Quiver presentations and Schur–Weyl duality for Khovanov arc algebras
C. Bowman, M. De Visscher, A. Dell’Arciprete, A. Hazi, R. Muth, C. Stroppel

Abstract
. We provide an Ext {\rm Ext} -quiver and relations presentation of the Khovanov arc algebras and prove a precise analogue of the Kleshchev–Martin conjecture in this setting.



1.
Introduction

The Khovanov arc algebras,
H^{m}_{n}
, were first introduced by Khovanov (in the case
m=n
) in his pioneering construction of homological knot invariants for tangles
[
Kho00
,
Kho02
]
. These homological knot invariants have subsequently been developed by Rasmussen and put to use in Piccirillo’s proof that the Conway knot is not slice
[
Ras10
,
Pic20
]
.
The Khovanov arc algebras and their quasi-hereditary covers,
K^{m}_{n}
,
have been studied from the point of view of
their cohomological and representation theoretic structure
[
BS10
,
BS11b
,
BS12a
,
BS12b
,
BW
]
,
symplectic geometry
[
MS22
]
,
and they provide the
exciting possibility of constructing algebraic invariants suitable for Crane–Frenkel’s approach to the smooth 4-dimensional Poincaré conjecture
[
Man
]
.

In previous work, we gave a quadratic presentation of the algebras
K^{m}_{n}
as
{\mathbb{Z}}
-algebras
in terms of Dyck path combinatorics, which upon base change to any field
\Bbbk

specialised to be the

{\rm Ext}
-quiver and relations for the
\Bbbk
-algebra
K^{m}_{n}

[
BDHS
]
;
we hence completely determined the representation theoretic structure
of
K^{m}_{n}

in an entirely characteristic-free manner.
In
[
BDV+
]
we further proved that over any field the algebra

K^{m}_{n}
is an
(|n-m|-1)
-faithful cover of
H^{m}_{n}
(in the sense of Rouquier
[
Rou08
]
) thus establishing a strong cohomological
connection between these algebras.
In this paper, we push the
quadratic presentation of
K^{m}_{n}

through this Schur–Weyl duality to provide
complete
{\rm Ext}
-quiver and relations presentations of
H^{m}_{n}
, hence completely determining the representation theoretic structure of the Khovanov arc algebras.
We will see that the
structure of the algebras
H^{m}_{n}
is not as rigid as that of their quasi-hereditary covers: the Khovanov arc algebras are cubic and their structure does depend, to some extent, on the characteristic,
p
, of the underlying field
\Bbbk
. We assume, without loss of generality, that
m\leqslant n
.

Theorem A
.

The
{\rm Ext}
-quiver of
H^{m}_{n}
has vertices labelled by the
partitions
\lambda
in an
(m\times n)
-rectangle such that
(m,m-1,\dots,2,1)\subseteq\lambda
.
The edges are labelled by addable and removable Dyck paths:
for
m=n
and
p\neq 2
there are
m
additional loops at
\lambda=(m^{a},(m-a)^{m-a})
for
1\leqslant a\leqslant m
;
for
m=n
and
p=2
every vertex has an additional loop.
The
\Bbbk
-algebra
H^{m}_{n}
is the quotient of the path algebra of its
{\rm Ext}
-quiver modulo relations (
6.1
) to (
6.7
).

Examples of these
{\rm Ext}
-quivers are given in
Figures

1
,
2
and
3
.

Schur–Weyl duality is a more general
phenomenon that relates Lie theoretic objects (such as categories
\mathcal{O}
of Lie algebras, or (quantum) reductive algebraic groups)
to interesting finite dimensional algebras (such as the group algebras of symmetric groups, Iwahori–Hecke algebras, and the Brauer and walled Brauer algebras).
Representation theorists have long endeavoured to pass cohomological information back-and-forth by way of these dualities.
A benchmark for our understanding (or lack thereof) for this
back-and-forth process
has been provided by the famous Kleshchev–Martin conjecture: this states that
simple modules for the symmetric group do not admit self-extensions (providing that the Schur algebra is at least a 0-faithful cover).
We prove that the exact analogue of this statement holds in our context.

Theorem B
.

Let
\Bbbk
be a field.
The

{\rm Ext}
-quiver of
H^{m}_{n}
is loop-free
if and only if

K^{m}_{n}
is an
i
-faithful quasi-hereditary cover for some
i\geqslant 0
if and only if
m\neq n
.

Figure 1.
The
{\rm Ext}
-quiver of
{H}^{3}_{3}
for
p\neq 2
.

Figure 2.
The
{\rm Ext}
-quiver of
H^{3}_{3}
for
p=2
.

Figure 3.
The
{\rm Ext}
-quiver of
H^{2}_{3}
for all
p\geqslant 2
.

The paper is structured as follows. Section 2 recalls the combinatorics of partitions and Dyck paths necessary for the statements of our results.
Sections 3 and 4 develops this Dyck combinatorics further, providing the language needed to discuss the simple heads of cell modules of
Khovanov arc algebras.
Section 5 recalls the definition of the Khovanov arc algebras, their quasi-hereditary covers
the extended Khovanov arc algebras
and
discusses their cellular structures; here we prove that every cell module has a simple head and deduce the full submodule structures of these cell modules.
In Section 6 we define an abstract
{\mathbb{Z}}
-algebra via Dyck combinatorial generators and relations and prove that this algebra projects surjectively onto the Khovanov arc algebra.
In Section 7 we show that this map is an isomorphism of algebras,
therefore the arc algebras inherit these
Dyck combinatorial presentations.
Finally, in Section 8 we make the few tweaks necessary to refine these presentations into
{\rm Ext}
-quiver and relations presentations over a field
\Bbbk
; as our algebras are basic, this simply requires that we find a minimal set of generators.
In Section 8 we also recouch the Kleshchev–Martin conjecture in our language and propose a vast generalisation of this conjecture to all anti-spherical Hecke categories.

Acknowledgements
.

The first and third authors were funded by EPSRC grant
EP/V00090X/1.

2.
Partitions, cup and cap diagrams, and
p
-Kazhdan-Lusztig polynomials

We begin by reviewing and unifying the combinatorics of Khovanov arc algebras

[
BS10
,
BS11b
,
BS12a
,
BS12b
]
and the Hecke categories of interest in this paper
[
BDHN
,
BDH+
]
.

Let
S_{n}
denote the symmetric group of degree
n
. Throughout this paper, we will work with the parabolic Coxeter system
(W,P)=(S_{m+n},S_{m}\times S_{n})
.
For the entire paper we assume, without loss of generality, that
m\leqslant n
.
We label the simple reflections with the slightly unusual subscripts
s_{i},\,-m+1\leqslant i\leqslant n-1
so that
P=\langle s_{i}\,|\,i\neq 0\rangle\leqslant W
. We view
W
as the group of permutations of the
n+m
points on a horizontal strip numbered by the half integers
i\pm\tfrac{1}{2}
where the simple reflection
s_{i}
swaps the points
i-\tfrac{1}{2}
and
i+\tfrac{1}{2}
and fixes every other point.
The right cosets of
P
in
W
can then be identified by labelled horizontal strips called
weights
, where each point
i\pm\tfrac{1}{2}
is labelled by either
\wedge
or
\vee
in such a way that the total number of
\wedge
is equal to
m
(and so the total number of
\vee
is equal to
n
). Specifically, the trivial coset
P
is represented by the weight with negative points labelled by
\wedge
and positive points labelled by
\vee
. The other cosets are obtained by permuting the labels of the identity weight. An example is given in
4
.
For more details on this combinatorics, see
[
BDHS
, Section 2]
.

\wedge

\wedge

\vee

\wedge

\vee

\vee

\wedge

\vee

\wedge

\wedge

\wedge

\wedge

\wedge

\wedge

\vee

\vee

\vee

\vee

Figure 4.

Along the top we picture the weight
\vee\wedge\vee\wedge\wedge\vee\wedge\vee\wedge\vee\in\Lambda_{5,5}
,
which corresponds to the partition
(5,4,2,1)\in\mathscr{P}_{5,5}
.
Filling each box in the partition with an
s_{i}
generator constructs the corresponding coset (which when applied to the minimal element of
\Lambda_{5,5}
permutes the
\wedge
s and
\vee
s so as to arrive at the given weight).

Formally, a
partition

\lambda
of
\ell
is defined to be a weakly decreasing sequence of non-negative integers
\lambda=(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2},\ldots)
which sum to
\ell
. We call
\ell(\lambda):=\ell=\sum_{i}\lambda_{i}
the length of the partition
\lambda
.
We define the Young diagram of a partition to be the collection of tiles

[\lambda]=\{[r,c]\mid 1\leqslant c\leqslant\lambda_{r}\}

depicted in Russian style with rows at
135^{\circ}
and columns at
45^{\circ}
. We identify a partition with its Young diagram.
We let
\lambda^{t}
denote the transpose partition given by reflection
of the Russian Young diagram through the vertical axis.
Given
m,n\in{\mathbb{N}}
we let
{\mathscr{P}}_{m,n}
denote the set of all partitions which fit into an
m\times n
rectangle, that is

{\mathscr{P}}_{m,n}=\{\lambda\mid\lambda_{1}\leqslant m,\lambda_{1}^{t}\leqslant n\}.

For
\lambda\in{\mathscr{P}}_{m,n}
, the
x
-coordinate of a tile
[r,c]\in\lambda
is equal to
r-c\in\{-m+1,-m+2,\ldots,n-2,n-1\}
and we define this
x
-coordinate to be the
content
of the tile and we write
{\sf cont}[r,c]=r-c
.
Given
\lambda\in{\mathscr{P}}_{m,n}
, we define the set
{\rm Add}(\lambda)
to be the set of all tiles
[r,c]\notin\lambda
such that
\lambda\cup[r,c]\in{\mathscr{P}}_{m,n}
. Similarly, we define the set
{\rm Rem}(\lambda)
to be the set of all tiles
[r,c]\in\lambda
such that
\lambda\setminus[r,c]\in{\mathscr{P}}_{m,n}
.

The following definitions come from
[
BS11a
]
.

Definition 2.1
.

\circ

To each weight
\lambda
we associate a
cup diagram

\underline{\lambda}
and a
cap diagram

\overline{\lambda}
. To construct
\underline{\lambda}
,
repeatedly find a pair of vertices labeled
\vee

\wedge
in order from left to right that are neighbours in the sense that there are only vertices already joined by cups in between. Join these new vertices together with a cup. Then repeat the process until there are no more such
\vee

\wedge
pairs.
Finally draw south-westerly rays at all the remaining
\wedge
vertices
and south-easterly rays at all the remaining
\vee
vertices.
The cap diagram
\overline{\lambda}
is obtained by flipping
\underline{\lambda}
horizontally. We stress that the vertices of the cup and cap diagrams are not labeled.

\circ

Let
\lambda
and
\mu
be weights. We can glue
\underline{\mu}
under
\lambda
to obtain a new diagram
\underline{\mu}\lambda
. We say that
\underline{\mu}\lambda
is
oriented
if (i) the vertices at the ends of each cup in
\underline{\mu}
are labelled by exactly one
\vee
and one
\wedge
in the weight
\lambda
and (ii) it is impossible to find two rays in
\underline{\mu}
whose top vertices are labeled
\vee

\wedge
in that order from left to right in the weight
\lambda
.
Similarly, we obtain a new diagram
\lambda\overline{\mu}
by gluing
\overline{\mu}
on top of
\lambda
. We say that
\lambda\overline{\mu}
is oriented if
\underline{\mu}\lambda
is oriented.

\circ

Let
\lambda
,
\mu
be weights such that
\underline{\mu}\lambda
is oriented. We set the
degree
of the diagram
\underline{\mu}\lambda
(respectively
\lambda\overline{\mu}
) to the the number of clockwise oriented cups (respectively caps) in the diagram.

\circ

Let
\lambda,\mu,\nu
be weights such that
\underline{\mu}\lambda
and
\lambda\overline{\nu}
are oriented. Then we form a new diagram
\underline{\mu}\lambda\overline{\nu}
by gluing
\underline{\mu}
under and
\overline{\nu}
on top of
\lambda
. We set
{\rm deg}(\underline{\mu}\lambda\overline{\nu})={\rm deg}(\underline{\mu}\lambda)+{\rm deg}(\lambda\overline{\nu})
.

An example is provided in Figure
5
.

For the purposes of this paper, for
p\geqslant 0
, we can define the
p
-Kazhdan–Lusztig polynomials of type
(W,P)=(S_{n+m},S_{m}\times S_{n})
as follows.
For
\lambda,\mu\in{\mathscr{P}_{m,n}}
we set

{{}^{p}}n_{\lambda,\mu}(q)=\begin{cases}q^{\deg(\underline{\mu}\lambda)}&\text{if $\underline{\mu}\lambda$ is oriented}\\
0&\text{otherwise.}\end{cases}

We refer to
[
BDH+
, Theorem 7.3]
and
[
BDHN
, Theorem A]
for a justification of this definition and to
[
BS11a
]
for the origins of this combinatorics.

\vee
\vee
\wedge
\wedge
\wedge
\vee
\vee
\wedge
\wedge
\vee
\vee

Figure 5.
The construction of the cup diagram
\underline{\lambda}
for
\lambda=(5,4,2^{2})\in\mathscr{P}_{5,6}
.

It is clear that for a fixed
\mu\in{\mathscr{P}_{m,n}}
, the diagram
\underline{\mu}\lambda
is oriented if and only if the weight
\lambda
is obtained from the weight
\mu
by swapping the labels on some of the pairs of vertices connected by a cup in
\underline{\mu}
. Moreover, in this case the degree of
\underline{\mu}\lambda
is precisely the number of such swapped pairs.

We define the
defect
of
\lambda\in\mathscr{P}_{m,n}
,
to be
d(\lambda)=d-m\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{\leqslant 0}
if
(d,d-1,d-2,\dots,1)\subseteq\lambda
but

(d+1,d,d-1,\dots,1)\not\subseteq\lambda
.
In other words, the defect records the largest staircase partition sitting inside of
\lambda
(relative to the largest staircase partition
(m,m-1,m-2,\dots,1)\in{\mathscr{P}_{m,n}}
).
We say that a partition
\lambda\in\mathscr{P}_{m,n}
is
regular
if
d(\lambda)=0
.
We let
\mathscr{R}_{m,n}\subseteq\mathscr{P}_{m,n}
denote the subset of regular partitions.
It follows from definitions that
\lambda\in{\mathscr{P}_{m,n}}
has defect

d(\lambda)\leqslant 0
if and only if
\underline{\lambda}\lambda
has precisely

|d(\lambda)|

vertices labelled by
\wedge
connected to south-westerly rays.
For example
the partitions
\lambda=(5,4,2^{2})
has defect
-1
, as seen in
Figure

5
.

3.
Dyck combinatorics

We have defined the
p
-Kazhdan–Lusztig polynomials via
counting of certain
oriented diagrams.
For the purposes of this paper, we require richer combinatorial objects which
refine
the diagrammatic construction: these are provided by tilings by Dyck paths.

Let us start with a simple example to see how these Dyck paths come from the oriented diagrams. Consider the partitions
\mu=(5^{3},4,1)
and
\lambda=(4^{2},3,1^{2})
. The oriented diagrams
\underline{\mu}\mu
and
\underline{\lambda}\lambda
are illustrated in
Figure

6
. We see that
\lambda
is obtained from
\mu
by swapping the labels of the vertices of one cup,
p\in\underline{\mu}
.
The partition
\lambda
is obtained from the partition
\mu
by removing a corresponding Dyck path
P\subseteq\mu
.
More generally, if
\lambda,\mu\in{\mathscr{P}_{m,n}}
with
\underline{\mu}\lambda
oriented of degree
k
, then we will see that the partition
\lambda
is obtained from the partition
\mu
by removing
k
Dyck paths.

\vee

\vee

\vee

Figure 6.
On the left we picture the partition/cup diagram for
(5^{3},4,1)
and we highlight the arc,
p
, and the corresponding
removable Dyck path
P
. On the right we have the
partition/cup diagram obtained by removing
P
.

3.1.
Dyck paths

We define a path on the
m\times n
tiled rectangle to
be a finite non-empty set
P

of tiles that are ordered
[r_{1},c_{1}],\ldots,[r_{s},c_{s}]
for some
s\geqslant 1
such that
for each
1\leqslant i\leqslant s-1
we have
[r_{i+1},c_{i+1}]=[r_{i}+1,c_{i}]
or
[r_{i},c_{i}-1]
. Note that the set
\underline{\sf cont}(P)
of contents of the tiles in a path
P
form an interval of integers.
We say that
P
is a
Dyck path
if

\min\{r_{i}+c_{i}-1\,:\,1\leqslant i\leqslant s\}=r_{1}+c_{1}-1=r_{s}+c_{s}-1,

that is, the minimal height of the path is achieved at the start and end of the path.
We will write

\displaystyle{\sf first}(P)={\sf cont}([r_{1},c_{1}])\qquad\textup{ and }\qquad{\sf last}(P)={\sf cont}([r_{s},c_{s}]).

We designate the
height

h(P)
and
breadth

b(P)
as:

\displaystyle{\sf ht}(P)=r_{1}+c_{1}-1-m=r_{s}+c_{s}-1-m\qquad\textup{ and }\qquad b(P)=\tfrac{1}{2}(|P|+1)

so that
h(P)
records the vertical position of the lowest nodes in
P
and
b(P)
records the horizontal distance covered by
P
.

Definition 3.1
.

Let
P
and
Q
be Dyck paths.

\circ

We say that
P
and
Q
are
adjacent
if and only if the multiset given by the disjoint union
{\sf cont}(P)\sqcup{\sf cont}(Q)
is an interval.

\circ

We say that
P
and
Q
are
distant
if and only if

\min\{|{\sf cont}[r,c]-{\sf cont}[x,y]|\,:\,[r,c]\in P,[x,y]\in Q\}\geqslant 2.

\circ

We say that
P

covers

Q
and write
Q\prec P
if and only if

{\sf first}(Q)>{\sf first}(P)\,\,\mbox{and}\,\,{\sf last}(Q)<{\sf last}(P).

Examples of such Dyck paths
P
and
Q
are given in Figure
7
.

Figure 7.
Examples of
\color[rgb]{1,0,1}P
and
\color[rgb]{0,1,1}Q
adjacent, distant, and
{\color[rgb]{0,1,1}Q}\prec\color[rgb]{1,0,1}P
respectively.

Now we fix a partition
\mu\in{\mathscr{P}_{m,n}}
.
Throughout the paper, we will identify all Dyck paths
P
having the same content interval
\underline{\sf cont}(P)
.
There are a few of places where we will need to fix a particular representative for a Dyck path
P
and in that case we will use subscripts, such as
P_{b}
.

Definition 3.2
.

Let
\mu\in{\mathscr{P}_{m,n}}
and
P
be a Dyck path. We say that
P
is a
removable Dyck path
from
\mu
if
there is a representative
P_{b}
of
P
such that
\lambda:=\mu\setminus P_{b}\in{\mathscr{P}_{m,n}}
.
In this case we will write
\lambda=\mu-P
, and
{\sf ht}^{\mu}(P):={\sf ht}(P_{b})
. We define the set
{\rm DRem}(\mu)
to be the set of all removable Dyck paths from
\mu
.

We say that
P
is an
addable Dyck path
of
\mu
if there is a representative
P_{b}
of
P
such that
\lambda:=\mu\sqcup P_{b}\in{\mathscr{P}_{m,n}}
. In this case we will write
\lambda=\mu+P
and
{\sf ht}^{\lambda}(P):=h(P_{b})
. We define the set
{\rm DAdd}(\mu)
to be the set of all addable Dyck paths of
\mu
.

We let
{\rm DRem}_{k}(\mu)
denote the set of all removable Dyck paths of
\mu
of height

k
and similarly
{\rm DRem}_{\geqslant k}(\mu)
,
{\rm DRem}_{\leqslant k}(\mu)
(and we define similarly
{\rm DAdd}_{k}(\mu)
et cetera).

A removable Dyck path
P\in{\rm DRem}(\mu)
corresponds to an anti-clockwise arc in the diagram
\underline{\mu}\mu
with endpoints at
{\sf first}(P)
and
{\sf last}(P)
. In particular, if
P_{b}
is the removable representative of
P\in{\rm DRem}(\mu)
, then its height
{\sf ht}^{\mu}(P)
is given by

\displaystyle{\sf ht}^{\mu}(P)=\#\{\vee\textup{ to the left of }{\sf first}(P)\textup{ in }\mu\}-\#\{\wedge\textup{ to the left of }{\sf first}(P)\textup{ in }\mu\}.

Definition 3.3
.

Let
\mu\in{\mathscr{P}_{m,n}}
and
P,Q\in{\rm DRem}(\mu)
. We say that
P
and
Q

commute
if
P\in{\rm DRem}(\mu-Q)
and
Q\in{\rm DRem}(\mu-P)
.

We wish to consider the effect of adding two Dyck paths, or subtracting one from the other.

Definition 3.4
.

Let
P\in{\rm DRem}(\mu)
and
Q\in{\rm DRem}(\mu-P)
be adjacent.
We define the
merge
of
P
and
Q
, denoted

\langle P\cup Q\rangle_{\mu}
, if it exists, to be the smallest removable Dyck path of
\mu
containing
P\cup Q
.

Definition 3.5
.

Let
P,Q\in{\rm DRem}(\mu)
be adjacent, with
P\prec Q
.
Then we define the
split
of
Q
by
P
, denoted
{\sf split}_{Q}(P)
, to be the Dyck tiling

Q\setminus P=Q^{1}\sqcup Q^{2}
where
Q^{1},Q^{2}\in{\rm DRem}(\mu-P)
.

Definition 3.6
.

Let
\lambda\subseteq\mu\in{\mathscr{P}_{m,n}}
. A
Dyck tiling
of the skew partition
\mu\setminus\lambda
is a set
\{P^{1},\dotsc,P^{k}\}
of Dyck paths such that

\mu\setminus\lambda=\bigsqcup_{i=1}^{k}P^{i}

and for each
i\neq j
we have either
P^{i}
covers
P^{j}
(or vice versa), or
P^{i}
and
P^{j}
are distant.
We call
(\lambda,\mu)
a
Dyck pair of degree
k
if
\mu\setminus\lambda
has a Dyck tiling with
k
Dyck paths.

The Dyck tiling
\{P^{1},\ldots,P^{k}\}
for
\mu\backslash\lambda
(if it exists) is unique, though there are generally distinct choices of representatives
\{P^{1}_{b},\ldots,P^{k}_{b}\}
for
\{P^{1},\ldots,P^{k}\}
(we will consider these choices in the next subsection). We will freely associate the skew partition
\mu\backslash\lambda
with its Dyck tiling, and abuse notation in using
\mu\backslash\lambda
to refer to the Dyck tiling as well.

3.2.
Dyck tableaux

A
good move
is a map

\underline{\mu}\lambda\to\underline{\nu}\lambda

such that
\deg(\underline{\nu}\lambda)=\deg(\underline{\mu}\lambda)-1
and is of one of
the following four possible forms.
The first case to consider is when

\nu=\mu-P
where
P\in{\rm DRem}(\mu)
and
d(\nu)=d(\mu)-1
,
in which case we can break the arc
p
into 2 strands as follows:

(G1)

The second case is that

\nu=\mu-P
where
P\in{\rm DRem}(\mu)
and

d(\mu)=d(\nu)
.
We first suppose that there does not exist
P\prec Q\in{\rm DRem}(\lambda)
, in which case we can do precisely one of the following good moves

(G2)

We now suppose that there does exist
P\prec Q\in{\rm DRem}(\lambda)
and suppose
b(Q)
is minimal with respect to this property.
We can further assume that there is no
P\prec R\prec Q
such that
R\in\mu/\lambda
. In which case we do the following good move

(G3)

Proposition 3.7
.

For any Dyck pair
(\lambda,\mu)
of degree
k
, there clearly exists a sequence of good moves

\underline{\mu}\lambda=\underline{\mu_{k}}\lambda\to\underline{\mu_{k-1}}\lambda\to\dots\to\underline{\mu_{0}}\lambda=\underline{\lambda}\lambda.

Proof.

We simply note that the only
apparent
restriction
for this process is that the (
G3
) good move requires
there not exist
P\prec R\prec Q
; however,
if there does exist such an
R
then we can consider the pair
(R,Q)
first.
∎

Definition 3.8
.

Let
(\lambda,\mu)
be a Dyck pair of degree
k
. A
Dyck tableau
for
(\lambda,\mu)
is any sequence of good moves

\underline{\mu}\lambda=\underline{\mu_{k}}\lambda\to\underline{\mu_{k-1}}\lambda\to\dots\to\underline{\mu_{0}}\lambda=\underline{\lambda}\lambda.

3.3.
Height of paths in a tiling

Let
(\lambda,\mu)
be a Dyck pair. Let
P
be a clockwise arc in
\underline{\mu}\lambda
.
We define the set of Dyck paths
supported
by
P\in\mu/\lambda

(and denote this set by
{\sf supp}^{\mu}_{\lambda}(P)
)
as follows. Extend a line downward from the lowest point of the arc
P
in
\underline{\mu}\lambda
which terminates as soon as it has intersected equal numbers of clockwise arcs (including
P)
as anti-clockwise arcs/half-arcs, or else never terminates. Then
{\sf supp}^{\mu}_{\lambda}(P)
is the set of clockwise arcs in
\underline{\mu}\lambda
intersected by this line.
We define the
height
of a Dyck path
P\in\mu/\lambda
as follows

(3.1)

\displaystyle{\sf ht}^{\mu}_{\lambda}(P)=\min\{{\sf ht}^{\mu}(P),{\sf ht}^{\mu}(Q)-1\mid Q\in{\sf supp}^{\mu}_{\lambda}(P)\backslash P\}.

Note that if
P\in{\rm DRem}(\mu)
then
{\sf ht}^{\mu}(P)={\sf ht}^{\mu}_{\mu-P}(P)
.

Proposition 3.9
.

Let
(P^{1},\ldots,P^{k})
be the unique ordering of the clockwise arcs in
\underline{\mu}\lambda
such that

\displaystyle{\sf ht}^{\mu}_{\lambda}(P^{i})<{\sf ht}^{\mu}_{\lambda}(P^{i+1})\qquad\textup{or}\qquad{\sf ht}^{\mu}_{\lambda}(P^{i})={\sf ht}^{\mu}_{\lambda}(P^{i+1})\textup{ and }{\sf last}(P^{i})<{\sf first}(P^{i+1}).

Then, setting
\mu_{i}=\mu_{i+1}-P^{i+1}
, we have that

(3.2)

\displaystyle\underline{\mu}\lambda=\underline{\mu_{k}}\lambda\to\underline{\mu_{k-1}}\lambda\to\dots\to\underline{\mu_{0}}\lambda=\underline{\lambda}\lambda

is a Dyck tableau.

Proof.

Let

P\prec Q\in{\rm DRem}(\lambda)
and assume
b(Q)
is minimal with respect to this property.
If

P\prec R\prec Q

for

R\in\mu/\lambda
then

R\in{\sf supp}^{\mu}_{\lambda}(P)
and so

{\sf ht}^{\mu}_{\lambda}(P)<{\sf ht}^{\mu}_{\lambda}(R)
. Therefore we can always remove any maximal height Dyck path using a good move.
∎

Figure 8.
The arc diagram of
\underline{\mu}\lambda
, for the partitions
\mu=(20^{2},18^{5},16^{7},11^{2},6^{3},2^{3})
and
\lambda=(18^{3},15,14^{3},13,10^{3},9,7^{2},5,2^{5})
. For each coloured clockwise arc
P
, the support set
{\sf supp}^{\mu}_{\lambda}(P)
is indicated by the set of coloured arcs intersected by the grey line emanating downward from
P
.
We record the height
{\sf ht}^{\mu}_{\lambda}(P)
(in the sense of
equation

3.1
) at the upward vertex of
P
(and we colour pink/blue if this height is positive/non-positive). Compare with
Figure

9
.

Figure 9.
A Dyck tiling for the Dyck pair
(\lambda,\mu)
as in
Figure

8
chosen so as to highlight the heights. The canonical tableau is the tableau which adds these specific Dyck paths in order from bottom-to-top and left-to-right.
The Dyck paths of strictly positive height are coloured

pink
and all others are coloured
blue
.
Compare with
Figure

8
.

Definition 3.10
.

We refer to the tableau of (
3.2
) as
the
canonical Dyck tableau
.
For
k\in{\mathbb{Z}}
we define

(\mu\setminus\lambda)_{k}=\{P\mid P\in\mu\setminus\lambda\text{ and }{\sf ht}^{\mu}_{\lambda}(P)=k\}

and we define
(\mu\setminus\lambda)_{\leqslant k}
and
(\mu\setminus\lambda)_{\geqslant k}
in a similar fashion.
See
Figure

9
for an example.

Remark 3.11
.

The canonical Dyck tiling corresponds to the unique choice of representatives
\{P_{b}^{1},\ldots,P_{b}^{k}\}
for the Dyck tiles
\{P^{1},\ldots,P^{k}\}

with the following property: for all
i,j
with
P^{i}\prec P^{j}
we have that
P^{i}_{b}
is below
P^{j}_{b}
only if there is no other choice of representatives
\{P_{b^{\prime}}^{1},\ldots,P_{b^{\prime}}^{k}\}
with
P^{i}_{b}
above
P^{j}_{b}
.

One may arrive at the canonical tiling for
\mu\backslash\lambda
from any choice of representatives for the Dyck tiling of
\mu\backslash\lambda
by iteratively commuting shorter Dyck tiles upwards past longer Dyck tiles whenever possible.
The acceptable arrangements for Dyck paths
\color[rgb]{0,1,1}P\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\prec\color[rgb]{1,0,1}Q
in a canonical tiling are captured
in
Figure

10
.

Figure 10.
The first two arrangements of Dyck paths are good and the final arrangement is bad.
This is because the small blue Dyck path in the third diagram can be commuted upwards to obtained the second diagram (thus turning the bad arrangement into a good one).
See
Figure

8
for an example.

4.
The regularisation map

We now define a regularisation map from non-regular partitions to regular partitions.
For any Dyck pair
(\alpha,\mu)
, we
then construct a sequence from
{\sf reg}(\alpha)
to

\mu
given by adding and splitting Dyck paths. This is the combinatorial shadow of a representation theoretic result:
in
Section

5
we will use this map to show that the
H^{m}_{n}
-cell-module
S_{m,n}(\alpha)
has simple head

D_{m,n}({\sf reg}(\alpha))
.

Definition 4.1
.

Given
\alpha\in{\mathscr{P}_{m,n}}
with
d(\alpha)=d<0
, we define the regularisation of
\alpha
, denoted
{\sf reg}(\alpha)
, as follows.
We define a sequence of partitions

(4.1)

\displaystyle\alpha={\sf reg}_{d}(\alpha)\subset{\sf reg}_{d+1}(\alpha)\subset\dots\subset{\sf reg}_{-1}(\alpha)\subset{\sf reg}_{0}(\alpha)={\sf reg}(\alpha)

such that
P^{k}={\sf reg}_{k}(\alpha)\setminus{\sf reg}_{k-1}(\alpha)
is the maximal breadth addable Dyck path of height
k
.
In other words,
P^{k}
is the Dyck path obtained by connecting all the nodes
[r,c]\not\in{\sf reg}_{k-1}(\alpha)
such that
r+c-1-m=k
.

From the arc diagram point of view,
\underline{{\sf reg}(\alpha)}\alpha
is the diagram obtained from
\underline{\alpha}\alpha
by
applying (the reverse of) as many
(G1)
good moves as possible, thus replacing all
|d(\alpha)|
south-westerly strands
(and therefore a corresponding
|d(\alpha)|
south-easterly strands) with cups.

Figure 11.
Three partitions of negative defect and their regularisations.

Remark 4.2
.

The regularisation process produces the canonical tiling

{\sf reg}(\alpha)\setminus\alpha=\bigsqcup\limits_{d(\alpha)+1\leqslant k\leqslant 0}P^{k}

of
{\sf reg}(\alpha)\setminus\alpha
and with
{\sf ht}(P^{k})=k\leqslant 0
for all
d(\alpha)+1\leqslant k\leqslant 0
.
Moreover, this is the unique tiling of
{\sf reg}(\alpha)\setminus\alpha
and is of degree
|d(\alpha)|
.

Example 4.3
.

Consider the partition
\alpha=(8,6^{2},2^{2},1^{2})
whose regularisation is the one in the centre of
Figure

11
. We have that
d(\alpha)=-3
and the sequence of partitions produced by the regularisation is:

\alpha={\sf reg}_{-3}(\alpha)\subset{\sf reg}_{-2}(\alpha)\subset{\sf reg}_{-1}(\alpha)\subset{\sf reg}_{0}(\alpha)={\sf reg}(\alpha)

where
{\sf reg}_{-2}(\alpha)={\sf reg}_{-3}(\alpha)+\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{P^{-2}}
,
{\sf reg}_{-1}(\alpha)={\sf reg}_{-2}(\alpha)+\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}{P^{-1}}
, and
{\sf reg}_{0}(\alpha)={\sf reg}_{-1}(\alpha)+\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}{P^{0}}
. Each
P^{k}
for
-2\leqslant k\leqslant 0
corresponds to the the Dyck path of the same colour in the Figure
13
. The corresponding sequence of oriented diagrams is pictured in
Figure

12

Figure 13.
The sequence of partitions produced by the regularisation of
\alpha=(8,6^{2},2^{2},1^{2})
.

Proposition 4.4
.

Let
\alpha\in{\mathscr{P}_{m,n}},\mu\in\mathscr{R}_{m,n}
. Assume
\mu\backslash\alpha=(\mu\backslash\alpha)_{\leqslant 0}
is a Dyck pair of degree
k
.
Then
(\mu,{\sf reg}(\alpha))
is a Dyck pair of degree
k+d(\alpha)
.

Proof.

Consider the diagrams
\underline{\alpha}\alpha
and
\underline{\mu}\alpha
.
We let
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
denote the set of arcs and strands common to both diagrams
\underline{\alpha}\alpha
and
\underline{\mu}\alpha
and we set
\color[rgb]{0,1,1}\mathcal{A}
to the set of arcs in
\underline{\mu}\alpha
not belonging to
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
.
Our assumption that
(\mu\backslash\alpha)_{\leqslant 0}=\mu\backslash\alpha
implies that
\underline{\alpha}\alpha
is obtained from
\underline{\mu}\alpha
by a sequence of good moves of the form (
G1
) and (
G2
). In particular, no end point of an arc in
\color[rgb]{0,1,1}\mathcal{A}
lies within a region above an arc or strand from
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
.

We let

\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\mathcal{B}
denote the set of arcs in

\underline{\alpha}\alpha
and which are not in
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
.
We let
\color[rgb]{1,0,1}\mathcal{C}

denote the set of strands in
\underline{\alpha}\alpha
which do not belong to
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
.
Since
\mu
is of defect zero,
the end points
of the arcs
in

\color[rgb]{0,1,1}\mathcal{A}
are in bijection with the end points of the arcs and strands in
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\mathcal{B}}\sqcup\color[rgb]{1,0,1}\mathcal{C}
.
We have that
\color[rgb]{1,0,1}\mathcal{C}
consists of
2|d(\alpha)|
strands (half south-easterly and half south-westerly) and that
\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\mathcal{B}
consists solely of anti-clockwise oriented cups.

We observe that
\underline{{\sf reg}(\alpha)}
is obtained from
\underline{\alpha}
by replacing all the strands in
\color[rgb]{1,0,1}\mathcal{C}
with cups.
Therefore
{{\sf reg}(\alpha)}
is obtained from

\alpha
by flipping the labels of the vertices at the end points
of the strands from
\color[rgb]{1,0,1}\mathcal{C}
.
On the other hand,
\mu
is obtained from
{\sf reg}(\alpha)
by flipping all
the labels of the vertices
at the end points
of the arcs in

\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\mathcal{B}
.

Therefore
\underline{{\sf reg}(\alpha)}{\mu}
is an oriented diagram of degree equal to the number of arcs in
\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\mathcal{B}
(which is equal to
k+d(\alpha)
) as required.
∎

\vee

\vee
\vee
\vee
\vee
\vee

Figure 14.

From left to right we picture

\underline{\mu}\alpha
,

\underline{\alpha}\alpha
,

\underline{{\sf reg}(\alpha)}\mu
. The sets
\color[rgb]{0,1,1}\mathcal{A}
,

\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\mathcal{B}
, and
\color[rgb]{1,0,1}\mathcal{C}
are coloured.

Instead of adding Dyck paths to get from
\lambda

to

{\sf reg}(\alpha)
, we can remove Dyck paths to get from
{\sf reg}(\alpha)
to
\lambda
. In fact, since
\mu/\alpha
is a Dyck tiling, these removals can
be thought of as splitting Dyck paths of
{\sf reg}(\alpha)/\alpha
. In particular, we have the following.

Corollary 4.5
.

Let
(\mu\setminus\alpha)_{\leqslant 0}
be a Dyck tiling with
\alpha\notin\mathscr{R}_{m,n}
. Then there is a sequence of partitions of the form

(4.2)

\displaystyle{\sf reg}(\alpha)={\sf split}_{d(\alpha)}(\mu\setminus\alpha)\supseteq{\sf split}_{d(\alpha)+1}(\mu\setminus\alpha)\dots\supseteq{\sf split}_{0}(\mu\setminus\alpha)=\alpha\sqcup(\mu\setminus\alpha)_{\leqslant 0}

with

({\sf split}_{k}(\mu\setminus\alpha))\setminus({\sf split}_{k-1}(\mu\setminus\alpha))=R_{1}^{k}\sqcup R_{2}^{k}\sqcup\dots\sqcup R_{K}^{k}

a disjoint union of commuting Dyck paths such that

P^{k}-R_{1}^{k}-R_{2}^{k}-\dots-R_{K}^{k}=(\mu\setminus\alpha)_{k}.

In particular,
R_{1}^{k},R_{2}^{k},\ldots,R_{K}^{k}
are ordered from left to right.

Remark 4.6
.

It’s worth noting that if
R_{i}^{k}
and
R_{j}^{\ell}
are such that
k<\ell
, then either
R_{i}^{k}
and
R_{j}^{\ell}
commute or
R_{i}^{k}\succ R_{j}^{\ell}
.

Example 4.7
.

Consider the Dyck tiling
\mu\setminus\alpha
on the left of
Figure

15
where
\alpha
is the partition of Example
4.3
.
We know the regularisation of
\alpha
from Example
4.3
. Following Corollary
4.5
, we have that our “favourite path” from
{\sf reg}(\alpha)\setminus\alpha
to
(\mu\setminus\alpha)_{\leqslant 0}
is depicted in
Figure

16
. With the notation introduced in Corollary
4.5
, we have that

{\sf reg}(\alpha)={\sf split}_{-3}(\mu\setminus\alpha)\supseteq{\sf split}_{-1}(\mu\setminus\alpha)\supseteq{\sf split}_{0}(\mu\setminus\alpha)=\alpha\sqcup(\mu\setminus\alpha)_{\leqslant 0}

and we have that

\circ

{\sf reg}(\alpha)=\alpha\sqcup{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}P^{-2}}\sqcup{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}P^{-1}}\sqcup{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}P^{0}}
;

\circ

there are no splitting at height
-2
, so
{\sf split}_{-2}(\mu\setminus\alpha)={\sf split}_{-3}(\mu\setminus\alpha)
;

\circ

{\sf split}_{-1}(\mu\setminus\alpha)\setminus{\sf split}_{-2}(\mu\setminus\alpha)=R^{-1}
and so
(\mu\setminus\alpha)_{-1}={\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}P^{-1}}-{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}R^{-1}}
;

\circ

{\sf split}_{0}(\mu\setminus\alpha)\setminus{\sf split}_{-1}(\mu\setminus\alpha)=R_{1}^{0}\sqcup R_{2}^{0}
and so
(\mu\setminus\alpha)_{0}={\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}P^{0}}-{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}R_{1}^{0}}-{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}R_{2}^{0}}
.

.
The Dyck tiling of

(\mu\setminus\alpha)_{\leqslant 0}
and

\mu\setminus\alpha
with
\mu=(8^{3},7,6^{3},4,2)
and
\alpha=(8,6^{2},2^{2},1^{2})

\end{minipage}

Figure 16
.
Our favourite path as in
Example

4.7
.

Remark 4.8
.

Let
\mu\setminus\alpha
be a Dyck tiling with
\alpha\notin\mathscr{R}_{m,n}
. Then there is a sequence of partitions of the form

(4.3)

\displaystyle(\mu\setminus\alpha)_{\leqslant 0}={\sf add}_{0}(\mu\setminus\alpha)\subseteq{\sf add}_{1}(\mu\setminus\alpha)\dots\subseteq{\sf add}_{k-1}(\mu\setminus\alpha)\subseteq{\sf add}_{k}(\mu\setminus\alpha)=\mu

where

{\sf add}_{k}(\mu\setminus\alpha)\setminus{\sf add}_{k-1}(\mu\setminus\alpha)=A_{1}^{k}\sqcup A_{2}^{k}\sqcup\dots\sqcup A_{K}^{k}

is a union of addable Dyck paths of height
k>0
.

Example 4.9
.

Consider the Dyck tiling
\mu\setminus\alpha=(8^{3},7,6^{3},4,2)\setminus\alpha=(8,6^{2},2^{2},1^{2})
depicted in
Figure

15
.
Notice that the Dyck tiling of
\mu\setminus\alpha
has a Dyck path of height
1
and that
(\mu\setminus\alpha)_{\leqslant 0}
is the Dyck tiling consider in Example
4.7
.

Algorithm 4.1
.

Let
(\alpha,\mu)
be a Dyck pair.
We construct a canonical sequence of add/splits from
\alpha\to\mu
by first adding Dyck paths as in
(
4.1
), then splitting Dyck paths as in (
4.2
), then adding Dyck paths as in (
4.3
).

An example of the three “big steps” in this algorithm is given in
Figure

17
, the smaller steps are given in
Figures

13
and
16
.

The sequence
\alpha\to{\sf reg}(\alpha)\to\alpha\sqcup(\mu\setminus\alpha)_{\leqslant 0}\to\mu\setminus\alpha
where the steps are regularise, split, add as in (
4.1
), (
4.2
), and (
4.3
). In the third diagram the boxes marked with an
\times
are the ones deleted in the splitting step.

5.
The (extended) Khovanov arc algebras, cellularity, and the Schur functor

We are now ready to introduce the algebras of interest in this paper and recall the facts we will require about their representation theory.
The new result of this section is that
all
cell modules of Khovanov arc algebras have simple heads (this makes use of the combinatorics developed in
Section

4
).
This allows us to give a new construction of the cellular basis of the Khovanov arc algebra; this will provide the backbone of our proof of Theorem A from the introduction.

5.1.
The original definitions of the (extended) Khovanov arc algebras

We now recall the definition of the extended Khovanov arc algebras studied in
[
BS10
,
BS11b
,
BS12a
,
BS12b
]
.
We define
K^{m}_{n}
to be the algebra spanned by diagrams

\{\underline{\lambda}\mu\overline{\nu}\mid\lambda,\mu,\nu\in{\mathscr{P}_{m,n}}\text{ such that }\mu\overline{\nu},\underline{\lambda}\mu\text{ are oriented}\}

with the multiplication defined as follows.
First set

(\underline{\lambda}\mu\overline{\nu})(\underline{\alpha}\beta\overline{\gamma})=0\quad\mbox{unless $\nu=\alpha$}.

To compute
(\underline{\lambda}\mu\overline{\nu})(\underline{\nu}\beta\overline{\gamma})
place
(\underline{\lambda}\mu\overline{\nu})
under
(\underline{\nu}\beta\overline{\gamma})
then follow the ‘surgery’ procedure.
This surgery combines two circles into one or splits one circle into two using the following rules for re-orientation (where we use the notation

1=\text{anti-clockwise circle}
,
x=\text{clockwise circle}
,
y=\text{oriented strand}
). We have the splitting rules

1\mapsto 1\otimes x+x\otimes 1,\quad x\mapsto x\otimes x,\quad y\mapsto x\otimes y.

and the merging rules

\displaystyle 1\otimes 1\mapsto 1,\quad 1\otimes x\mapsto x,\quad x\otimes 1\mapsto x,\quad x\otimes x\mapsto 0,\quad 1\otimes y\mapsto y,\quad x\otimes y\mapsto 0,

\displaystyle y\otimes y\mapsto\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}y\otimes y&\text{if both strands are propagating,
one is
}\\
&\text{$\wedge$-oriented and the other is $\vee$-oriented;}\\
0&\text{otherwise.}\end{array}\right.

.

\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}} \end{minipage}

where we highlight with arrows the pair of arcs on which we are about to perform surgery. The first equality follows from the merging rule for
1\otimes 1\mapsto 1
and the second equality follows from the merging rule

1\mapsto 1\otimes x+x\otimes 1
.

where we highlight with arrows the pair of arcs on which we are about to perform surgery.
This is similar to
Example

5.1
.

5.2.
Quiver presentations and cellularity of the extended Khovanov arc algebra

In
[
BDHS
]
we proved that the
extended
Khovanov arc algebras are isomorphic to the basic algebras of the category algebras of Hecke categories of type
({S}_{m+n},{S}_{m}\times{S}_{n})
, see also
[
BDHS24
]
. This isomorphism was constructed in a similar spirit to that of
[
BCH23
,
BCHM22
]
.
These isomorphisms allowed us to prove an integral presentation of the extended Khovanov arc algebras
in
[
BDHS
, Theorem B]
, which we now recall.

Definition 5.3
.

The algebra
K^{m}_{n}
is the associative
\Bbbk
-algebra generated by the elements

(5.1)

\{D^{\lambda}_{\mu},D_{\lambda}^{\mu}\mid\text{$\lambda,\mu\in{\mathscr{P}_{m,n}}$ with $\lambda=\mu-P$ for some $P\in{\rm DRem}(\mu)$}\}\cup\{{\sf 1}_{\mu}\mid\mu\in{\mathscr{P}_{m,n}}\}

subject to the following relations and their duals.

The idempotent
relations:

For all
\lambda,\mu\in{\mathscr{P}_{m,n}}
, we have that

(5.2)

{\sf 1}_{\mu}{\sf 1}_{\lambda}=\delta_{\lambda,\mu}{\sf 1}_{\lambda}\qquad\qquad{\sf 1}_{\lambda}D^{\lambda}_{\mu}{\sf 1}_{\mu}=D^{\lambda}_{\mu}.

The
self-dual relation:

Let
P\in{\rm DRem}(\mu)
and
\lambda=\mu-P
. Then we have

(5.3)

D_{\mu}^{\lambda}D_{\lambda}^{\mu}=(-1)^{b(P)-1}\Bigg(2\!\!\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}Q\in{\rm DRem}(\lambda)\\
P\prec Q\end{subarray}}\!\!(-1)^{b(Q)}D^{\lambda}_{\lambda-Q}D^{\lambda-Q}_{\lambda}+\!\!\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}Q\in{\rm DRem}(\lambda)\\
Q\,\text{adj.}\,P\end{subarray}}\!\!(-1)^{b(Q)}D^{\lambda}_{\lambda-Q}D^{\lambda-Q}_{\lambda}\Bigg)

where we abbreviate “adjacent to” simply as “adj.”

The commuting relations:

Let
P,Q\in{\rm DRem}(\mu)
which commute. Then we have

(5.4)

D^{\mu-P-Q}_{\mu-P}D^{\mu-P}_{\mu}=D^{\mu-P-Q}_{\mu-Q}D^{\mu-Q}_{\mu}\qquad D^{\mu-P}_{\mu}D^{\mu}_{\mu-Q}=D^{\mu-P}_{\mu-P-Q}D^{\mu-P-Q}_{\mu-Q}.

The non-commuting relation:

Let
P,Q\in{\rm DRem}(\mu)
with
P\prec Q
which do not commute. Then
Q\setminus P=Q^{1}\sqcup Q^{2}
where
Q^{1},Q^{2}\in{\rm DRem}(\mu-P)
and we have

(5.5)

D^{\mu-Q}_{\mu}D^{\mu}_{\mu-P}=D^{\mu-Q}_{\mu-P-Q^{1}}D^{\mu-P-Q^{1}}_{\mu-P}=D^{\mu-Q}_{\mu-P-Q^{2}}D^{\mu-P-Q^{2}}_{\mu-P}

The adjacent relation:

Let
P\in{\rm DRem}(\mu)
and
Q\in{\rm DRem}(\mu-P)
be adjacent. Recall that

\langle P\cup Q\rangle_{\mu}
, if it exists, denotes the smallest removable Dyck path of
\mu
containing
P\cup Q
. Then we have

(5.6)

D^{\mu-P-Q}_{\mu-P}D^{\mu-P}_{\mu}=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}(-1)^{b(\langle P\cup Q\rangle_{\mu})-b(Q)}D^{\mu-P-Q}_{\mu-\langle P\cup Q\rangle_{\mu}}D^{\mu-\langle P\cup Q\rangle_{\mu}}_{\mu}&\mbox{if $\langle P\cup Q\rangle_{\mu}$ exists}\\
0&\mbox{otherwise}\end{array}\right.

Definition 5.4
.

Given a Dyck tiling

\mu\setminus\lambda=\bigsqcup_{i=1}^{k}P^{i}

we define an associated diagram

D^{\lambda}_{\mu}=D^{\lambda}_{\lambda+P^{1}}D^{\lambda+P^{1}}_{\lambda+P^{1}+P^{2}}\dots D^{\lambda+P^{1}+\dots+P^{k-1}}_{\lambda+P^{1}+\dots+P^{k-1}+P^{k}}

which is independent of the ordering in which the Dyck paths are added.

The following construction is due to Libedinsky–Williamson
[
LW
]
, but is couched in our language in
[
BDHS
, Theorem 5.4]
.

Theorem 5.5

(
[
BDHS
, Theorem 5.4]
and
[
LW
]
)
.

The algebra

K^{m}_{n}
is a graded cellular (in fact quasi-hereditary) algebra with graded cellular basis given by

(5.7)

\{D_{\lambda}^{\mu}D^{\lambda}_{\nu}\mid\lambda,\mu,\nu\in{\mathscr{P}_{m,n}}\,\,\mbox{with}\,\,(\lambda,\mu),(\lambda,\nu)\text{ Dyck pairs}\}

with

{\rm deg}(D_{\lambda}^{\mu}D^{\lambda}_{\nu})={\rm deg}(\lambda,\mu)+{\rm deg}(\lambda,\nu),

with respect to the involution
*
and the partial order on
{\mathscr{P}_{m,n}}
given by inclusion.

Definition 5.6
.

For
\lambda\in{\mathscr{P}_{m,n}}
, we define the
standard module

\Delta_{m,n}(\lambda)
to be the right
K^{m}_{n}
-module

\Delta_{m,n}(\lambda)={\sf 1}_{\lambda}K^{m}_{n}/(K^{m}_{n}(\textstyle\sum_{\mu\subset\lambda}{\sf 1}_{\mu})K^{m}_{n}).

This module has basis
\{D^{\lambda}_{\nu}\mid(\lambda,\nu)\text{ is a Dyck pair}\}
with action given by right concatenation (modulo the ideal spanned by diagrams which factor through
\mu\subset\lambda
).

Proposition 5.7
.

A complete and irredundant set of non-isomorphic simple
K^{m}_{n}
-modules
is given by

\{L_{m,n}(\lambda)\mid L_{m,n}(\lambda)=\Delta_{m,n}(\lambda)/{\rm rad}(\Delta_{m,n}(\lambda)),\text{ for }\lambda\in\mathscr{P}_{m,n}\}.

We now describe the full submodule structure of the standard modules.
As
K^{m}_{n}
is positively graded, the grading provides a submodule filtration of
\Delta_{m,n}(\lambda)
. Decompose
{\rm DP}(\lambda)
as

{\rm DP}(\lambda)=\bigsqcup_{k\geqslant 0}{\rm DP}_{k}(\lambda)\quad\text{where}\quad{\rm DP}_{k}(\lambda)=\{\mu\in{\rm DP}(\lambda)\,:\,\deg(\lambda,\mu)=k\}.

Theorem 5.8
.

Let
\lambda\in{\mathscr{P}_{m,n}}
. The Alperin diagram of the standard module
\Delta_{m,n}(\lambda)
has vertex set labelled by the set

\{L_{m,n}(\mu)\,:\,\mu\in{\rm DP}(\lambda)\}
and edges

L_{m,n}(\mu)\longrightarrow L_{m,n}(\nu)

whenever
\mu\in{\rm DP}_{k}(\lambda)
,
\nu\in{\rm DP}_{k+1}(\lambda)
for some
k\geqslant 0
and
\nu=\mu\pm P
for some
P\in{\rm DAdd}(\mu)
or
P\in{\rm DRem}(\mu)
respectively.

An example is depicted in
Figure

18
, below.

Figure 18.
The full submodule lattice of the Verma module
\Delta_{3,3}(2,1)
for

K_{3,3}
and
\Bbbk
any field.
We represent each simple module by the corresponding partition (in Russian notation) and highlight the
3\times 3
rectangle in which the partition exists.
This module has simple head
L_{3,3}(2,1)
and simple socle
L_{3,3}(3,2,1)
.
Each edge connects a pair of partitions which differ by adding or removing a single Dyck path.

5.3.
The Schur functor

We set

\mathscr{R}_{m,n}=\{\lambda\in\mathscr{P}_{m,n}\mid\lambda\text{ has defect zero}\}.

We define the
Schur idempotent
to be the element

e=\sum_{\lambda\in\mathscr{R}_{m,n}}{\sf 1}_{\lambda}\in K^{m}_{n}.

The main object of study in this paper will be the subalgebras

eK^{m}_{n}e\subseteq K^{m}_{n}

for
m,n\in{\mathbb{N}}
.
We have the following immediate results:

Corollary 5.9
.

The algebra

eK^{m}_{n}e
is a graded cellular algebra with graded cellular basis given by

(5.8)

\{D_{\lambda}^{\mu}D^{\lambda}_{\nu}\mid\lambda\in{\mathscr{P}_{m,n}},\mu,\nu\in\mathscr{R}_{m,n}\,\,\mbox{with}\,\,(\lambda,\mu),(\lambda,\nu)\text{ Dyck pairs}\}

with

{\rm deg}(D_{\lambda}^{\mu}D^{\lambda}_{\nu})={\rm deg}(\lambda,\mu)+{\rm deg}(\lambda,\nu),

with respect to the involution
*
and the partial order on
{\mathscr{P}_{m,n}}
given by inclusion.

Proof.

This follows from
Theorem

5.5
and the definition of these algebras by idempotent truncation.
∎

Definition 5.10
.

For
\lambda\in{\mathscr{P}_{m,n}}
we define the
Specht module

S_{m,n}(\lambda)
to be the right
H^{m}_{n}
-module

S_{m,n}(\lambda)=\Delta_{m,n}(\lambda)e

this module has basis
\{D^{\lambda}_{\nu}\mid(\lambda,\nu)\text{ is a Dyck pair},\nu\in\mathscr{R}_{m,n}\}
with action given by right concatenation (modulo the ideal spanned by diagrams which factor through
\mu\subset\lambda
).

Proposition 5.11
.

A complete and irredundant set of non-isomorphic simple
H^{m}_{n}
-modules
is given by

\{D_{m,n}(\lambda)\mid D_{m,n}(\lambda)=L_{m,n}(\lambda)e,\text{ for }\lambda\in\mathscr{R}_{m,n}\subset{\mathscr{P}_{m,n}}\}.

Proof.

By
Theorem

5.5
(which implies that
\mathscr{P}_{m,n}
labels the simple
K^{m}_{n}
-modules)
we need only show that

L_{m,n}(\lambda)e=0
if and only if
\lambda\not\in\mathscr{R}_{m,n}
.
This claim follows immediately from the fact that the algebra
K^{m}_{n}
is basic (and so all simple modules are 1-dimensional and generated by weight idempotents) and the definition of
e\in K^{m}_{n}
as the sum over all weight idempotents from
\mathscr{R}_{m,n}
.
∎

Proposition 5.12
.

Let
\lambda\in{\mathscr{P}_{m,n}}
. The Alperin diagram of the Specht module
S_{m,n}(\lambda)
has vertex set labelled by the set

\{D_{m,n}(\mu)\,:\,\mu\in{\rm DP}(\lambda)\cap\mathscr{R}_{m,n}\}
and edges

D_{m,n}(\mu)\longrightarrow D_{m,n}(\nu)

whenever
\mu\in{\rm DP}_{k}(\lambda)
,
\nu\in{\rm DP}_{k+1}(\lambda)
for some
k\geqslant 0
and
\nu=\mu\pm P
for some
P\in{\rm DAdd}(\mu)
or
P\in{\rm DRem}(\mu)
respectively.

Proof.

This follows from
Theorem

5.8
and the definition of these algebras by idempotent truncation.
∎

An example is depicted in
Figure

19
, below.

Figure 19.
The full submodule lattice of the Specht module
S_{3,3}(2,1)
for

eK_{3,3}e
and
\Bbbk
any field.
This is obtained from the diagram in
Figure

18
by simply deleting all composition factors labelled by

\lambda\not\in\mathscr{R}_{m,n}
(and edges from these vertices).

Thus we have complete submodule structure of the Specht modules, for free. In particular, we have the following:

Proposition 5.13
.

The Specht module
S_{m,n}(\alpha)
has simple head isomorphic to
D({\sf reg}(\alpha))\langle d(\alpha)\rangle
.

Proof.

This follows from
Proposition

5.12
and
algorithm

4.1
.
∎

Proposition 5.14
.

The algebra

eK^{m}_{n}e
is a graded cellular algebra with graded cellular basis given by

(5.9)

\{D_{{\sf reg}(\alpha)}^{\mu}D^{{\sf reg}(\alpha)}_{\alpha}D_{{\sf reg}(\alpha)}^{\alpha}D^{{\sf reg}(\alpha)}_{\nu}\mid\alpha\in{\mathscr{P}_{m,n}},\mu,\nu\in\mathscr{R}_{m,n}\,\,\mbox{with}\,\,(\alpha,\mu),(\alpha,\nu)\text{ Dyck pairs}\}

with

{\rm deg}(D_{\alpha}^{\mu}D^{\alpha}_{\nu})={\rm deg}(\alpha,\mu)+{\rm deg}(\alpha,\nu),

with respect to the involution
*
and the partial order on
{\mathscr{P}_{m,n}}
given by inclusion.

Proof.

This follows from
Proposition

5.12
and
algorithm

4.1
.
∎

6.
A symmetric algebra defined via Dyck combinatorics

We now define an abstract algebra,
\mathcal{A}_{m,n}
via Dyck-combinatorial
generators and relations. The main result of this paper will be that this algebra is isomorphic
as a
{\mathbb{Z}}
-graded
\Bbbk
-algebra
to
eK^{m}_{n}e
. We will prove this result in two steps over the next two chapters: we will first provide a spanning set of
\mathcal{A}_{m,n}
in terms of pairs of Dyck paths; we will then construct a surjective
\Bbbk
-algebra homomorphism
\mathcal{A}_{m,n}\to H^{m}_{n}
. Putting these two facts together, we will deduce that the spanning set is in fact a basis and the homomorphism is injective, as required.
However, before we can get on with the task of defining
\mathcal{A}_{m,n}
, we first require some additional combinatorics.

Definition 6.1
.

We define the
regular quiver

Q_{m,n}
with vertex set

\{\mathbbm{1}_{\lambda}\mid\lambda\in\mathscr{R}_{m,n}\}
and
arrows

\circ

\mathbb{D}^{\lambda}_{\mu}:\lambda\to\mu

and

\mathbb{D}^{\mu}_{\lambda}:\mu\to\lambda
for every
\lambda=\mu-P
with
P\in{\rm DRem}_{>0}(\mu)
;

\circ

for
m=n
we have additional “loops” of degree 2,

\mathbb{L}^{\lambda}_{\lambda}:\lambda\to\lambda
for every
\lambda\in\mathscr{R}_{m,m}
.

An example is depicted in
Figure

20
below.

Figure 20.
The regular quiver
{Q}_{2,4}
is obtained by “doubling up” the above graph.

Definition 6.2
.

Given
P,Q
two Dyck paths, we say that
P
is
(right) dominated by

Q
, and write
P\lhd Q
if

{\sf last}(P)<{\sf first}(Q)
.
Given
P\in{\rm Rem}_{0}(\lambda)
, we let
{\sf rt}(P)
denote the element of

\{Q\mid P\vartriangleleft Q,Q\in{\rm Add}_{1}(\lambda)\}
which is of maximal breadth.

We define certain generalised loop elements of the path algebra
\Bbbk Q_{m,n}
as follows.
We first associate the loop generator to a canonical Dyck path as follows:

Definition 6.3
.

For
\lambda\in\mathscr{R}_{m,n}
and
P\in{\rm DRem}(\lambda)
, we define an element
\mathbb{L}^{\lambda}_{\lambda}(-P)
as a product of the generators of the path algebra as follows

\displaystyle\mathbb{L}^{\lambda}_{\lambda}(-P)=\begin{cases}(-1)^{b(P)}\mathbb{D}^{\lambda}_{\lambda-P}\mathbb{D}_{\lambda}^{\lambda-P}&\text{if }{\sf level}(P)>0\\[5.0pt]
(-1)^{b({\sf rt}(P))+1}\mathbb{D}^{\lambda}_{\lambda+{\sf rt}(P)}\mathbb{D}_{\lambda}^{\lambda+{\sf rt}(P)}&\text{if }{\sf level}(P)=0\text{ and }m<n\\[5.0pt]
-\mathbb{L}^{\lambda}_{\lambda}+(-1)^{b({\sf rt}(P))+1}\mathbb{D}^{\lambda}_{\lambda+{\sf rt}(P)}\mathbb{D}_{\lambda}^{\lambda+{\sf rt}(P)}&\text{if }{\sf level}(P)=0\text{ and }m=n\\
\mathbb{L}^{\lambda}_{\lambda}&\text{if }{\sf level}(P)=0,\text{ $m=n$, ${\sf last}(P)=m-1$ }\end{cases}

Definition 6.4
.

We define the symmetric Dyck algebra,
\mathcal{A}_{m,n}
, to be the
{\mathbb{Z}}
-graded
\Bbbk
-algebra given by the path algebra

\Bbbk Q_{m,n}
modulo the following relations and their duals:

The idempotent
relations:

For all
\lambda,\mu\in{\mathscr{R}_{m,n}}
, we have that

(6.1)

\mathbbm{1}_{\mu}{\mathbbm{1}}_{\lambda}=\delta_{\lambda,\mu}{\mathbbm{1}}_{\lambda}\qquad\qquad{\mathbbm{1}}_{\lambda}\mathbb{D}^{\lambda}_{\mu}{\mathbbm{1}}_{\mu}=\mathbb{D}^{\lambda}_{\mu}\qquad{\mathbbm{1}}_{\lambda}\mathbb{L}^{\lambda}_{\lambda}{\mathbbm{1}}_{\lambda}=\mathbb{L}^{\lambda}_{\lambda}

(where the final relation is only defined for the
m=n
case).

The
self-dual relation:

Let
\lambda\in{\mathscr{R}_{m,n}}
and
P\in{\rm DAdd}(\lambda)
. Then we have

(6.2)

\mathbb{D}_{\lambda+P}^{\lambda}\mathbb{D}_{\lambda}^{\lambda+P}=(-1)^{b(P)-1}\Bigg(2\!\!\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}Q\in{\rm DRem}(\lambda)\\
P\prec Q\end{subarray}}\!\!\mathbb{L}^{\lambda}_{\lambda}(-Q)+\!\!\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}Q\in{\rm DRem}(\lambda)\\
Q\,\text{adj.}\,P\end{subarray}}\!\!\mathbb{L}^{\lambda}_{\lambda}(-Q)\Bigg)

where the elements on the righthand-side are linear combinations of the generators, as in
Definition

6.3
.

The commuting relations:

Let
P,Q\in{\rm DRem}(\lambda)
which commute. Then we have

(6.3)

\mathbb{D}^{\lambda-P-Q}_{\lambda-P}\mathbb{D}^{\lambda-P}_{\lambda}=\mathbb{D}^{\lambda-P-Q}_{\lambda-Q}\mathbb{D}^{\lambda-Q}_{\lambda}\qquad\mathbb{D}^{\lambda-P}_{\lambda}\mathbb{D}^{\lambda}_{\lambda-Q}=\mathbb{D}^{\lambda-P}_{\lambda-P-Q}\mathbb{D}^{\lambda-P-Q}_{\lambda-Q}.

The non-commuting relation:

Let
P,Q\in{\rm DRem}(\mu)
with
P\prec Q
which do not commute. Then
Q\setminus P=Q^{1}\sqcup Q^{2}
where
Q^{1},Q^{2}\in{\rm DRem}(\mu-P)
and we have

(6.4)

\mathbb{D}^{\mu-Q}_{\mu}\mathbb{D}^{\mu}_{\mu-P}=\mathbb{D}^{\mu-Q}_{\mu-P-Q^{1}}\mathbb{D}^{\mu-P-Q^{1}}_{\mu-P}=\mathbb{D}^{\mu-Q}_{\mu-P-Q^{2}}\mathbb{D}^{\mu-P-Q^{2}}_{\mu-P}

The adjacent relation:

Let
P\in{\rm DRem}(\mu)
and
Q\in{\rm DRem}(\mu-P)
be adjacent. Recall that

\langle P\cup Q\rangle_{\mu}
, if it exists, denotes the smallest removable Dyck path of
\mu
containing
P\cup Q
. Then we have

(6.5)

\mathbb{D}^{\mu-P-Q}_{\mu-P}\mathbb{D}^{\mu-P}_{\mu}=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}(-1)^{b(\langle P\cup Q\rangle_{\mu})-b(Q)}\mathbb{D}^{\mu-P-Q}_{\mu-\langle P\cup Q\rangle_{\mu}}\mathbb{D}^{\mu-\langle P\cup Q\rangle_{\mu}}_{\mu}&\mbox{if $\langle P\cup Q\rangle_{\mu}$ exists}\\
0&\mbox{otherwise}\end{array}\right.

The cubic relation:

Let
P\in{\rm DAdd}_{1}(\mu)
be such
{\sf last}(P)
is maximal with respect to this property. Then

(6.6)

\mathbb{D}_{\mu}^{\mu+P}\mathbb{D}^{\mu}_{\mu+P}\mathbb{D}^{\mu}_{\mu+P}=\begin{cases}(-1)^{b(P)+1}2\mathbb{L}^{\mu+P}_{\mu+P}\mathbb{D}^{\mu+P}_{\mu}&\text{if $m=n$}\\
0&\text{if $m<n$}.\end{cases}

The additional
m=n
relations:

We have the loop-nilpotency and loop-commutation relations: namely, for all
\lambda,\mu\in\mathscr{R}_{m,m}
the following holds

(6.7)

(\mathbb{L}^{\mu}_{\mu})^{2}=0\qquad\mathbb{D}^{\lambda}_{\mu}\mathbb{L}^{\mu}_{\mu}=\mathbb{L}^{\lambda}_{\lambda}\mathbb{D}^{\lambda}_{\mu}.

7.
The isomorphism theorem

In this section, we prove the main result of this paper: that the algebras
\mathcal{A}_{m,n}
and

K^{m}_{n}
are isomorphic as
{\mathbb{Z}}
-graded
\Bbbk
-algebras.
We first fix some notation as follows:

\mathbb{D}(-Q):=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}{\lambda\in\mathscr{R}_{m,n}}\\
Q\in{\rm DRem}(\lambda)\end{subarray}}\!\!\!{\mathbbm{1}}_{\lambda}\mathbb{D}^{\lambda}_{\lambda-Q}\qquad\mathbb{D}(+Q):=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}{\lambda\in\mathscr{R}_{m,n}}\\
Q\in{\rm DAdd}(\lambda)\end{subarray}}\!\!\!{\mathbbm{1}}_{\lambda}\mathbb{D}^{\lambda}_{\lambda+Q}\qquad\mathbb{L}(-Q):=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}{\lambda\in\mathscr{R}_{m,n}}\\
Q\in{\rm DRem}(\lambda)\end{subarray}}\!\!\!{\mathbbm{1}}_{\lambda}\mathbb{L}^{\lambda}_{\lambda}(-Q).

7.1.
Implied relations in the symmetric Dyck path algebra

In this section we go through the not inconsiderable effort of establishing
a number of lemmas and propositions regarding the products of elements from

\mathcal{A}_{m,n}
.

Lemma 7.1
.

Let
m<n
. For
Q\in{\rm DRem}_{0}(\lambda)
and
P\in{\rm DRem}_{>0}(\lambda)
we have that
either
P
and
Q
commute or

Q\setminus P=Q^{1}\sqcup Q^{2}
where
Q^{1},Q^{2}\in{\rm DRem}(\lambda-P)
.
In the former case we have that

\mathbb{L}^{\lambda}_{\lambda}(-Q)\mathbb{D}^{\lambda}_{\lambda-P}=\mathbb{D}^{\lambda}_{\lambda-P}\mathbb{L}^{\lambda-P}_{\lambda-P}(-Q)

and in the latter case we have that

(7.1)

\displaystyle\mathbb{D}^{\lambda}_{\lambda-P}\mathbb{L}^{\lambda-P}_{\lambda-P}(-Q^{1})=\mathbb{L}^{\lambda}_{\lambda}(-Q)\mathbb{D}^{\lambda}_{\lambda-P}=\mathbb{D}^{\lambda}_{\lambda-P}\mathbb{L}^{\lambda-P}_{\lambda-P}(-Q^{2}).

Proof.

We recall from
Definition

6.3
that

\mathbb{L}^{\lambda}_{\lambda}(-Q)=(-1)^{b({\sf rt}(Q))}\mathbb{D}^{\lambda}_{\lambda+{\sf rt}(Q)}\mathbb{D}_{\lambda}^{\lambda+{\sf rt}(Q)}.

If
P,Q
commute, then
P,{\sf rt}(Q)
commute as well. This is straightforward if
P
is to the left of
Q
. If
P
is to the right of
Q
, then
our assumptions
Q\in{\rm DRem}_{0}(\lambda)
and
P\in{\rm DRem}_{>0}(\lambda)

together imply that

{\sf first}(P)\geqslant{\sf last}(Q)+2
.
By definition
{\sf rt}(Q)\in{\rm DAdd}_{1}(\lambda)
and so

{\sf first}({\sf rt}(Q))\leqslant{\sf first}(P)-2

and either

{\sf last}({\sf rt}(Q))\leqslant{\sf first}(P)-2
or
{\sf last}({\sf rt}(Q))\geqslant{\sf last}(P)+2
; in either case,
P

and
{\sf rt}(Q)
commute.
Examples of these two cases are depicted in
Figure

21
.
Therefore we have that

\displaystyle\mathbb{L}^{\lambda}_{\lambda}(-Q)\mathbb{D}^{\lambda}_{\lambda-P}

\displaystyle=(-1)^{b({\sf rt}(Q))}{\mathbbm{1}}_{\lambda}\mathbb{D}(+{\sf rt}(Q))\mathbb{D}(-{\sf rt}(Q))\mathbb{D}(-P)

\displaystyle=(-1)^{b({\sf rt}(Q))}{\mathbbm{1}}_{\lambda}\mathbb{D}(+{\sf rt}(Q))\mathbb{D}(-P)\mathbb{D}(-{\sf rt}(Q))

\displaystyle=(-1)^{b({\sf rt}(Q))}{\mathbbm{1}}_{\lambda}\mathbb{D}(-P)\mathbb{D}(+{\sf rt}(Q))\mathbb{D}(-{\sf rt}(Q))

\displaystyle=\mathbb{D}^{\lambda}_{\lambda-P}\mathbb{L}^{\lambda-P}_{\lambda-P}(-Q).

where only the second and third equalities follows from applying the commuting relation (
6.3
) to the Dyck paths
P
and
{\sf rt}(Q)
; the first and fourth equalities follow by
Definition

6.3
.

Figure 21.
Examples of commuting paths
\color[rgb]{0,1,1}P
,
\color[rgb]{1,0,1}Q

and
\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}{\sf rt}({Q})

such that
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}Q}\in{\rm DRem}_{0}(\lambda)
and
{\color[rgb]{0,1,1}P}\in{\rm DRem}_{>0}(\lambda)
for
\lambda=(5^{2},3^{3},1)
and
\lambda=(5^{2},3^{3})
respectively.

We first consider the latter equality in
equation

7.1
.
We now suppose that
Q\setminus P=Q^{1}\sqcup Q^{2}
where
Q^{1},Q^{2}\in{\rm DRem}(\lambda-P)
and without loss of generality, we assume that

Q^{1}
is to the left of
P
and
Q^{2}
is to the right of
P
.
In which case,
{\sf rt}(Q)={\sf rt}(Q^{2})
and this Dyck path commutes with
P
.
We have that

\displaystyle\mathbb{D}^{\lambda}_{\lambda-P}\mathbb{L}^{\lambda-P}_{\lambda-P}(-Q)

\displaystyle=(-1)^{b({\sf rt}(Q))}{\mathbbm{1}}_{\lambda}\mathbb{D}(-P)\mathbb{D}(+{\sf rt}(Q))\mathbb{D}(-{\sf rt}(Q))

\displaystyle=(-1)^{b({\sf rt}(Q))}{\mathbbm{1}}_{\lambda}\mathbb{D}(+{\sf rt}(Q))\mathbb{D}(-P)\mathbb{D}(-{\sf rt}(Q))

\displaystyle=(-1)^{b({\sf rt}(Q))}{\mathbbm{1}}_{\lambda}\mathbb{D}(+{\sf rt}(Q))\mathbb{D}(-{\sf rt}(Q))\mathbb{D}(-P)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{L}^{\lambda}_{\lambda}(-Q)\mathbb{D}^{\lambda}_{\lambda-P}.

where the first and final equalities follow from
Definition

6.3
and our observation that
{\sf rt}(Q)={\sf rt}(Q^{2})
;
the second and third equalities follow from applying the commuting relation (
6.3
) to the Dyck paths
P
and
{\sf rt}(Q)
.

Figure 22.

An example of

Q,{\color[rgb]{0,1,1}P}\in{\rm DRem}_{1}(\lambda)
for
\lambda=(6^{2},5^{2},3,1)

such that

Q-{\color[rgb]{0,1,1}P}={\color[rgb]{1,0,1}Q^{1}}\sqcup{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}Q^{2}}
.
On the left we depict
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}{\sf rt}(Q)}={\sf rt}({\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}Q^{2}})
.
On the right we depict
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{\sf rt}(Q^{1})}
which we note is the smallest Dyck path
in
{\rm DAdd}(\lambda-P)

containing
both

{\sf rt}({\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}Q^{2}})
and
{\color[rgb]{0,1,1}P}
.

We now consider the former equality in
equation

7.1
.
We have that
{\sf rt}(Q^{1})
is the smallest Dyck path in

{\rm Add}_{1}(\lambda-P)
such that
P\prec{\sf rt}(Q^{1})
and
{\sf rt}(Q)\prec{\sf rt}(Q^{1})
.
We further note that
Q^{2}\prec{\sf rt}(Q^{1})
and that

b({\sf rt}(Q^{1}))=b({\sf rt}(Q))+b(Q^{2})+b(P)
.
See
Figure

22
for examples.
We have that

\displaystyle\mathbb{D}^{\lambda}_{\lambda-P}\mathbb{L}^{\lambda-P}_{\lambda-P}(-Q^{1})

\displaystyle=(-1)^{b({\sf rt}(Q^{1}))}{\mathbbm{1}}_{\lambda}\mathbb{D}(-P)\mathbb{D}(+{\sf rt}(Q^{1}))\mathbb{D}(-{\sf rt}(Q^{1}))

\displaystyle=(-1)^{2b({\sf rt}(Q^{1}))-b({\sf rt}(Q^{2}))}{\mathbbm{1}}_{\lambda}\mathbb{D}(+{\sf rt}(Q^{2}))\mathbb{D}(+Q^{2})\mathbb{D}(-{\sf rt}(Q^{1}))

\displaystyle=(-1)^{b({\sf rt}(Q^{2}))}{\mathbbm{1}}_{\lambda}\mathbb{D}(+{\sf rt}(Q^{2}))\mathbb{D}(-{\sf rt}(Q^{2}))\mathbb{D}(-P)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{L}^{\lambda}_{\lambda}(-Q)\mathbb{D}^{\lambda}_{\lambda-P}.

where the second equality follows from the adjacency relation (
6.5
)
applied to

{\sf rt}(Q^{2})\in{\rm Add}(\lambda)
and

Q^{2}\in{\rm Add}(\lambda+{\sf rt}(Q^{1}))
;
the third equality follows from relation (
6.4
) applied to
the non-commuting pair
Q^{2}\prec{\sf rt}(Q^{1})
; the first and fourth equalities follow from
Definition

6.3
.
∎

Lemma 7.2
.

Let
m=n
. For
Q\in{\rm DRem}_{0}(\lambda)
and
P\in{\rm DRem}_{>0}(\lambda)
we have that
either
P
and
Q
commute or

Q\setminus P=Q^{1}\sqcup Q^{2}
where
Q^{1},Q^{2}\in{\rm DRem}(\lambda-P)
.
In the former case we have that

(7.2)

\displaystyle\mathbb{L}^{\lambda}_{\lambda}(-Q)\mathbb{D}^{\lambda}_{\lambda-P}=\mathbb{D}^{\lambda}_{\lambda-P}\mathbb{L}^{\lambda-P}_{\lambda-P}(-Q)

and in the latter case we have that

(7.3)

\displaystyle\mathbb{D}^{\lambda}_{\lambda-P}\mathbb{L}^{\lambda-P}_{\lambda-P}(-Q^{1})=\mathbb{L}^{\lambda}_{\lambda}(-Q)\mathbb{D}^{\lambda}_{\lambda-P}=\mathbb{D}^{\lambda}_{\lambda-P}\mathbb{L}^{\lambda-P}_{\lambda-P}(-Q^{2}).

Proof.

We first consider the case that
{\sf last}(Q)=m-1
,
that is, the case
that
\mathbb{L}^{\lambda}_{\lambda}(-Q)=\mathbb{L}^{\lambda}_{\lambda}
. We first consider the subcase in which

P
and
Q
commute.
We have that
\mathbb{L}^{\lambda-P}_{\lambda-P}(-Q)=\mathbb{L}^{\lambda-P}_{\lambda-P}
and by relation (
6.7
) it follows that

\mathbb{L}^{\lambda}_{\lambda}(-Q)\mathbb{D}^{\lambda}_{\lambda-P}=\mathbb{L}^{\lambda}_{\lambda}\mathbb{D}^{\lambda}_{\lambda-P}=\mathbb{D}^{\lambda}_{\lambda-P}\mathbb{L}^{\lambda-P}_{\lambda-P}=\mathbb{D}^{\lambda}_{\lambda-P}\mathbb{L}^{\lambda-P}_{\lambda-P}(-Q).

Now we consider the subcase in which
P\in{\rm DRem}(\lambda)
is such that
Q\setminus P=Q^{1}\sqcup Q^{2}
where
Q^{1},Q^{2}\in{\rm DRem}(\lambda-P)
.
Without loss of generality, we suppose that
Q^{1}
is to the left of
Q^{2}
and therefore

{\sf last}(Q^{2})=m-1
. Thus,
\mathbb{L}^{\lambda-P}_{\lambda-P}(-Q^{2})=\mathbb{L}^{\lambda-P}_{\lambda-P}
and by relation (
6.7
) it follows that

\mathbb{L}^{\lambda}_{\lambda}(-Q)\mathbb{D}^{\lambda}_{\lambda-P}=\mathbb{L}^{\lambda}_{\lambda}\mathbb{D}^{\lambda}_{\lambda-P}=\mathbb{D}^{\lambda}_{\lambda-P}\mathbb{L}^{\lambda-P}_{\lambda-P}=\mathbb{D}^{\lambda}_{\lambda-P}\mathbb{L}^{\lambda-P}_{\lambda-P}(-Q^{2}).

Now, we observe that our assumptions on
P
and
Q
imply that
{\sf rt}(Q^{1})=P
.
Thus by
Definition

6.3
we have that

\mathbb{L}^{\lambda-P}_{\lambda-P}(-Q^{1})=-\mathbb{L}^{\lambda-P}_{\lambda-P}-(-1)^{b(P)}\mathbb{D}_{\lambda}^{\lambda-P}\mathbb{D}_{\lambda-P}^{\lambda},

which we input into the lefthand-side of
equation

7.3
and hence obtain

\displaystyle\mathbb{D}^{\lambda}_{\lambda-P}\mathbb{L}^{\lambda-P}_{\lambda-P}(-Q^{1})

\displaystyle={\mathbbm{1}}_{\lambda}\mathbb{D}(-P)(-\mathbb{L}^{\lambda-P}_{\lambda-P}-(-1)^{b(P)}\mathbb{D}(+P)\mathbb{D}(-P))

\displaystyle=-{\mathbbm{1}}_{\lambda}\mathbb{D}(-P)\mathbb{L}^{\lambda-P}_{\lambda-P}-2(-1)^{2b(P)+1}{\mathbbm{1}}_{\lambda}\mathbb{L}^{\lambda}_{\lambda}\mathbb{D}(-P)

\displaystyle=-{\mathbbm{1}}_{\lambda}\mathbb{L}^{\lambda}_{\lambda}\mathbb{D}(-P)+2{\mathbbm{1}}_{\lambda}\mathbb{L}^{\lambda}_{\lambda}\mathbb{D}(-P)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{L}^{\lambda}_{\lambda}\mathbb{D}^{\lambda}_{\lambda-P}

\displaystyle=\mathbb{L}^{\lambda}_{\lambda}(-Q)\mathbb{D}^{\lambda}_{\lambda-P}.

where the first and final equalities follow from
Definition

6.3
; the second equality follows from
relation (
6.6
); the third follows from applying relation (
6.7
) to the lefthand term and tidying-up the signs for the righthand term; the fourth equality is trivial.

It remain to consider the case in which
{\sf last}(Q)<m-1
. By
Definition

6.3
we can express these loops in terms of
{\sf rt}(Q)
and the case already considered above, as follows:

\mathbb{L}^{\lambda}_{\lambda}(-Q)=-\mathbb{L}^{\lambda}_{\lambda}-(-1)^{b({\sf rt}(Q))}\mathbb{D}^{\lambda}_{\lambda+{\sf rt}(Q)}\mathbb{D}_{\lambda}^{\lambda+{\sf rt}(Q)}.

Notice that
{\sf rt}(Q)\in{\rm DAdd}_{1}(\lambda)
and
P\in{\rm DRem}_{>0}(\lambda)
and so they cannot be adjacent. Thus,
the pair of Dyck path
{\sf rt}(Q)
and
P
commute. We have that

\displaystyle\mathbb{L}^{\lambda}_{\lambda}(-Q)\mathbb{D}^{\lambda}_{\lambda-P}

\displaystyle={\mathbbm{1}}_{\lambda}\big(-\mathbb{L}^{\lambda}_{\lambda}-(-1)^{b({\sf rt}(Q))}\mathbb{D}(+{\sf rt}(Q))\mathbb{D}(-{\sf rt}(Q))\big)\mathbb{D}(-P)

\displaystyle=-{\mathbbm{1}}_{\lambda}\mathbb{D}(-P)\mathbb{L}^{\lambda-P}_{\lambda-P}-(-1)^{b({\sf rt}(Q))}{\mathbbm{1}}_{\lambda}\mathbb{D}(+{\sf rt}(Q))\mathbb{D}(-{\sf rt}(Q))\mathbb{D}(-P)

\displaystyle=-{\mathbbm{1}}_{\lambda}\mathbb{D}(-P)\mathbb{L}^{\lambda-P}_{\lambda-P}-(-1)^{b({\sf rt}(Q))}{\mathbbm{1}}_{\lambda}\mathbb{D}(-P)\mathbb{D}(+{\sf rt}(Q))\mathbb{D}(-{\sf rt}(Q))

\displaystyle=-{\mathbbm{1}}_{\lambda}\mathbb{D}(-P)\big(\mathbb{L}^{\lambda-P}_{\lambda-P}-(-1)^{b({\sf rt}(Q))}\mathbb{D}(+{\sf rt}(Q))\mathbb{D}(-{\sf rt}(Q))\big)

where the first equality follows from
Definition

6.3
; the second follows from the loop-commutation relation (
6.7
); the third follows from the commuting relation applied to the Dyck paths
{\sf rt}(Q)
and
P
; the fourth equality follows from re-bracketing.
Finally, we observe that if
P\prec Q
, then
{\sf rt}(Q^{2})={\sf rt}(Q)
for
Q^{2}\in{\rm DRem}(\lambda-P)
;
whereas if
P
and
Q
commute we have that
Q\in{\rm DRem}(\lambda-P)
(and so we do not need to rewrite anything).
Therefore we conclude that

\displaystyle\mathbb{L}^{\lambda}_{\lambda}(-Q)\mathbb{D}^{\lambda}_{\lambda-P}

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}{\mathbbm{1}}_{\lambda}\mathbb{D}(-P)\mathbb{L}(-Q)&\text{if }Q,P\text{ commute;}\\
{\mathbbm{1}}_{\lambda}\mathbb{D}(-P)\mathbb{L}(-Q^{2})&\text{otherwise}\end{cases}.

We hence deduce that
equation

7.2
and the righthand equality of
equation

7.3
both hold.

It remains to verify the lefthand equality in
equation

7.3
.
We have that
{\sf rt}(Q^{1})
is the smallest Dyck path in

{\rm Add}_{1}(\lambda-P)
such that
P\prec{\sf rt}(Q^{1})
and
{\sf rt}(Q)\prec{\sf rt}(Q^{1})
.
We further note that
Q^{2}\prec{\sf rt}(Q^{1})
and that

b({\sf rt}(Q^{1}))=b({\sf rt}(Q))+b(Q^{2})+b(P)
.
See
Figure

23
for examples.
We have that

\displaystyle\mathbb{D}^{\lambda}_{\lambda-P}\mathbb{L}^{\lambda-P}_{\lambda-P}(-Q^{1})

\displaystyle={\mathbbm{1}}_{\lambda}\mathbb{D}(-P)\big(-\mathbb{L}^{\lambda-P}_{\lambda-P}-(-1)^{b({\sf rt}(Q^{1}))}\mathbb{D}(+{\sf rt}(Q^{1}))\mathbb{D}(-{\sf rt}(Q^{1})\big)

\displaystyle=-\mathbb{L}^{\lambda}_{\lambda}\mathbb{D}^{\lambda}_{\lambda-P}-(-1)^{b({\sf rt}(Q^{1}))}\mathbb{D}(-P)\mathbb{D}(+{\sf rt}(Q^{1}))\mathbb{D}(-{\sf rt}(Q^{1})

\displaystyle=-\mathbb{L}^{\lambda}_{\lambda}\mathbb{D}^{\lambda}_{\lambda-P}-(-1)^{2b({\sf rt}(Q^{1}))-b({\sf rt}(Q))}{\mathbbm{1}}_{\lambda}\mathbb{D}(+{\sf rt}(Q))\mathbb{D}(+Q^{2})\mathbb{D}(-{\sf rt}(Q^{1}))

\displaystyle=-\mathbb{L}^{\lambda}_{\lambda}\mathbb{D}^{\lambda}_{\lambda-P}-(-1)^{b({\sf rt}(Q))}{\mathbbm{1}}_{\lambda}\mathbb{D}(+{\sf rt}(Q))\mathbb{D}(-{\sf rt}(Q))\mathbb{D}(-P)

\displaystyle=(-\mathbb{L}^{\lambda}_{\lambda}-(-1)^{b({\sf rt}(Q))}\mathbb{D}(+{\sf rt}(Q))\mathbb{D}(-{\sf rt}(Q))\mathbb{D}_{\lambda-P}^{\lambda}

\displaystyle=\mathbb{L}^{\lambda}_{\lambda}(-Q)\mathbb{D}^{\lambda}_{\lambda-P}.

where the
first and final equalities follow by
Definition

6.3

and the second and third equalities follows from relations (
6.5
) and (
6.4
) (exactly as in the
m\neq n
case).
∎

Figure 23.
An example of

Q,{\color[rgb]{0,1,1}P}\in{\rm DRem}_{1}(\lambda)
for
\lambda=(6^{2},5^{2},3,1)

such that

Q-{\color[rgb]{0,1,1}P}={\color[rgb]{1,0,1}Q^{1}}\sqcup{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}Q^{2}}
.
On the left we depict
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}{\sf rt}(Q)}={\sf rt}({\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}Q^{2}})
.
On the right we depict
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{\sf rt}(Q^{1})}
which we note is the smallest Dyck path
in
{\rm DAdd}(\lambda-P)

containing
both

{\sf rt}({\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}Q^{2}})
and
{\color[rgb]{0,1,1}P}
.

We observe that the previous two lemmas covered the
m=n
and
m\neq n
cases separately.
Both proofs were very similar, but it was the

m=n
case that was more intricate. In what follows, we prove the results for the
m=n
case and leave adapting these arguments to
the easier
m\neq n
case as an exercise for the reader.

Proposition 7.3
.

Let
Q\in{\rm DRem}_{0}(\lambda)
and
P\in{\rm DAdd}_{>0}(\lambda)
.
If
P
and
Q
commute,
we have that

(7.4)

\displaystyle\mathbb{L}^{\lambda}_{\lambda}(-Q)\mathbb{D}^{\lambda}_{\lambda+P}

\displaystyle=\mathbb{D}^{\lambda}_{\lambda+P}\mathbb{L}^{\lambda+P}_{\lambda+P}(-Q).

If
P
and
Q
are adjacent, we have that

(7.5)

\displaystyle\mathbb{L}^{\lambda}_{\lambda}(-Q)\mathbb{D}^{\lambda}_{\lambda+P}

\displaystyle=\mathbb{D}^{\lambda}_{\lambda+P}\mathbb{L}^{\lambda+P}_{\lambda+P}(-\langle P\cup Q\rangle_{\lambda+P})

Proof.

We focus on the
m=n
case (the
m\neq n
case is similar, but easier).
As in the proof of
Lemma

7.2
, we must separate out according to the two distinct cases
for
\mathbb{L}^{\lambda}_{\lambda}(-Q)
in
Definition

6.3
.

Case 1.
We first suppose that

{\sf last}(Q)=m-1
where, by
Definition

6.3
, we have that

\mathbb{L}^{\lambda}_{\lambda}(-Q)=\mathbb{L}^{\lambda}_{\lambda}.

We first consider (
7.4
), in which

P
and
Q
are commuting Dyck paths.
In this case
Q\in{\rm DRem}(\lambda-P)
. Then

\displaystyle\mathbb{L}^{\lambda}_{\lambda}(-Q)\mathbb{D}^{\lambda}_{\lambda+P}

\displaystyle=\mathbb{L}^{\lambda}_{\lambda}\mathbb{D}^{\lambda}_{\lambda+P}=\mathbb{D}^{\lambda}_{\lambda+P}\mathbb{L}^{\lambda+P}_{\lambda+P}=\mathbb{D}^{\lambda}_{\lambda+P}\mathbb{L}^{\lambda+P}_{\lambda+P}(-Q).

by the loop-commutation relation (
6.7
) and
Definition

6.3
.

Now, suppose that
P,Q
are adjacent. In which case,
\langle P\cup Q\rangle_{\lambda+P}
is the rightmost removable Dyck path of
\lambda+P
(that is
{\sf last}(\langle P\cup Q\rangle_{\lambda+P})=m-1
).
We hence have that

\displaystyle\mathbb{L}^{\lambda}_{\lambda}(-Q)\mathbb{D}^{\lambda}_{\lambda+P}

\displaystyle=\mathbb{L}^{\lambda}_{\lambda}\mathbb{D}^{\lambda}_{\lambda+P}=\mathbb{D}^{\lambda}_{\lambda+P}\mathbb{L}^{\lambda+P}_{\lambda+P}=\mathbb{D}^{\lambda}_{\lambda+P}\mathbb{L}^{\lambda+P}_{\lambda+P}(-\langle P\cup Q\rangle_{\lambda+P})

by the loop-commutation relation (
6.7
).

Case 2.
For the remainder of the proof, we can assume that

{\sf last}(Q)<m-1
and so

\mathbb{L}^{\lambda}_{\lambda}(-Q)=-\mathbb{L}^{\lambda}_{\lambda}-(-1)^{b({\sf rt}(Q))}{\mathbbm{1}}_{\lambda}\mathbb{D}(+{\sf rt}(Q))\mathbb{D}(-{\sf rt}(Q))

by
Definition

6.3
.
Then

\displaystyle\mathbb{L}^{\lambda}_{\lambda}(-Q)\mathbb{D}^{\lambda}_{\lambda+P}

\displaystyle={\mathbbm{1}}_{\lambda}(-\mathbb{L}^{\lambda}_{\lambda}-(-1)^{b({\sf rt}(Q))}\mathbb{D}(+{\sf rt}(Q))\mathbb{D}(-{\sf rt}(Q)))\mathbb{D}(+P)

(7.6)

\displaystyle=-{\mathbbm{1}}_{\lambda}\mathbb{D}(+P)\mathbb{L}^{\lambda+P}_{\lambda+P}-(-1)^{b({\sf rt}(Q))}{\mathbbm{1}}_{\lambda}\mathbb{D}(+{\sf rt}(Q))\mathbb{D}(-{\sf rt}(Q))\mathbb{D}(+P)

where the second equality follows from the loop-commutation relation (
6.7
). We now need to consider the commuting and adjacent case separately (and focus on the latter term on the righthand-side of (
7.6
)).

Case 2: Commuting subcase.

The first case is that in which
P,Q
commute.
We must refine this further into two subcases: that in which
P
and
{\sf rt}(Q)
are a commuting pair of Dyck paths, and that in which
P
and
{\sf rt}(Q)
do not commute.

We first suppose that
P
and
{\sf rt}(Q)
commute (as in the leftmost example in
Figure

24
).
In this case the latter term on the righthand-side of (
7.6
) is as follows

(7.7)

\displaystyle{\mathbbm{1}}_{\lambda}\mathbb{D}(+{\sf rt}(Q))\mathbb{D}(-{\sf rt}(Q))\mathbb{D}(+P)

\displaystyle={\mathbbm{1}}_{\lambda}\mathbb{D}(+P)\mathbb{D}(+{\sf rt}(Q))\mathbb{D}(-{\sf rt}(Q))

by two applications of the commuting relation (
6.3
).
Substituting (
7.7
) into (
7.6
) we obtain

\displaystyle\mathbb{L}^{\lambda}_{\lambda}(-Q)\mathbb{D}^{\lambda}_{\lambda+P}

\displaystyle=-{\mathbbm{1}}_{\lambda}\mathbb{D}(+P)\mathbb{L}^{\lambda+P}_{\lambda+P}-(-1)^{b({\sf rt}(Q))}{\mathbbm{1}}_{\lambda}\mathbb{D}(+P)\mathbb{D}(+{\sf rt}(Q))\mathbb{D}(-{\sf rt}(Q))

\displaystyle={\mathbbm{1}}_{\lambda}\mathbb{D}(+P)(-\mathbb{L}^{\lambda+P}_{\lambda+P}-(-1)^{b({\sf rt}(Q))}{\mathbbm{1}}_{\lambda}\mathbb{D}(+{\sf rt}(Q))\mathbb{D}(-{\sf rt}(Q)))

\displaystyle=\mathbb{D}^{\lambda}_{\lambda+P}\mathbb{L}^{\lambda+P}_{\lambda+P}(-Q)

as required.

Figure 24.
Examples of commuting Dyck paths
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}Q}\in{\rm DRem}_{0}(\lambda)
and

{\color[rgb]{0,1,1}P}\in{\rm DAdd}_{1}(\lambda)
for
\lambda=(6^{2},4,3,2,1)
.
In the former case
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{\sf rt}(Q)}\in{\rm DAdd}_{1}(\lambda)
and
{\color[rgb]{0,1,1}P}\in{\rm DAdd}_{1}(\lambda)
commute, in the latter case they do not.

We continue with our assumption that
P
and
Q

commute, but now suppose that
P,{\sf rt}(Q)
do not commute.
This implies that
{\sf last}(P)={\sf last}({\sf rt}(Q))
and such that

P\prec{\sf rt}(Q)
as illustrated in the rightmost diagram in
Figure

24
.
In which case,
P\in{\rm DAdd}_{1}(\lambda)
and there exists

S\in{\rm DRem}_{2}(\lambda+{\sf rt}(Q))
such that

{\sf split}_{S}({\sf rt}(Q))=T\sqcup P

for some
T\in{\rm DAdd}_{1}(\lambda)
.
We have that

\displaystyle\mathbb{D}(+{\sf rt}(Q))\mathbb{D}(-{\sf rt}(Q))\mathbb{D}(+P)

\displaystyle=(-1)^{b({\sf rt}(Q))-b(T)}{\mathbbm{1}}_{\lambda}\mathbb{D}(+{\sf rt}(Q))\mathbb{D}(-S)\mathbb{D}(-T)

(7.8)

\displaystyle=(-1)^{b({\sf rt}(Q))-b(T)}{\mathbbm{1}}_{\lambda}\mathbb{D}(+P)\mathbb{D}(+T)\mathbb{D}(-T)

where the first equality follows from applying relation (
6.5
)
to the pair of adjacent Dyck paths

S,T

and the second equality follows by applying relation (
6.4
)
to the pair of non-commuting Dyck paths

S\prec{\sf rt}(Q)
.
Substituting (
7.8
) into (
7.6
) we obtain the following

\displaystyle\mathbb{L}^{\lambda}_{\lambda}(-Q)\mathbb{D}^{\lambda}_{\lambda+P}

\displaystyle=-\mathbb{D}^{\lambda}_{\lambda+P}\mathbb{L}^{\lambda+P}_{\lambda+P}-(-1)^{2b({\sf rt}(Q))-b(T)}{\mathbbm{1}}_{\lambda}\mathbb{D}(+P)\mathbb{D}(+T)\mathbb{D}(-T)

\displaystyle={\mathbbm{1}}_{\lambda}\mathbb{D}(+P)(-\mathbb{L}^{\lambda+P}_{\lambda+P}-(-1)^{b(T)}\mathbb{D}(+T)\mathbb{D}(-T))

\displaystyle=\mathbb{D}^{\lambda}_{\lambda+P}\mathbb{L}^{\lambda+P}_{\lambda+P}(-Q)

where the final equality follows since
T={\sf rt}(Q)
in
\lambda+P
.

Case 2: Adjacent subcase.

For the remainder of the proof we suppose that
P,Q
are a pair of adjacent Dyck paths (in particular
P\in{\rm DAdd}_{1}(\lambda)
).
There are three subcases to consider:

(i)
the Dyck paths
P
and
{\sf rt}(Q)
do not commute

(ii)
the Dyck paths
P
and
{\sf rt}(Q)
do commute

(iii)
the case
P={\sf rt}(Q)
.
These are depicted in
Figure

25
.

Subcase
(i)
. We first assume that the pair
P
and
{\sf rt}(Q)
do not commute.
Or equivalently, that
{\sf first}(P)={\sf last}(Q)+1
and
b(P)
is not maximal with respect to this property (when
b(P)
maximal we are in case
(iii)
).
An example of this is depicted in the leftmost diagram in
Figure

25
.
In this case, there exists
S\in{\rm DRem}_{2}(\lambda+{\sf rt}(Q))

such that

{\sf split}_{S}({\sf rt}(Q))=T\sqcup P

for some
T\in{\rm DAdd}_{1}(\lambda)
.
We have that

\displaystyle{\mathbbm{1}}_{\lambda}\mathbb{D}(+{\sf rt}(Q))\mathbb{D}(-{\sf rt}(Q))\mathbb{D}(+P)

\displaystyle=(-1)^{b({\sf rt}(Q))-b(T)}{\mathbbm{1}}_{\lambda}\mathbb{D}(+{\sf rt}(Q))\mathbb{D}(-S)\mathbb{D}(-T)

(7.9)

\displaystyle=(-1)^{b({\sf rt}(Q))-b(T)}{\mathbbm{1}}_{\lambda}\mathbb{D}(+P)\mathbb{D}(+T)\mathbb{D}(-T)

where the first equality follows by applying relation (
6.5
)
to the adjacent pair
S,T

and the second follows by relation (
6.4
) to the non-commuting pair
S\prec{\sf rt}(Q)
.
Substituting (
7.9
) into (
7.6
) we obtain the following

\displaystyle\mathbb{L}^{\lambda}_{\lambda}(-Q)\mathbb{D}^{\lambda}_{\lambda+P}

\displaystyle=-{\mathbbm{1}}_{\lambda}\mathbb{D}(+P)\mathbb{L}^{\lambda+P}_{\lambda+P}-(-1)^{2b({\sf rt}(Q))-b(T)}{\mathbbm{1}}_{\lambda}\mathbb{D}(+P)\mathbb{D}(+T)\mathbb{D}(-T)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{D}^{\lambda}_{\lambda+P}(-\mathbb{L}^{\lambda+P}_{\lambda+P}-(-1)^{b(T)}\mathbb{D}(+T)\mathbb{D}(-T))

\displaystyle=\mathbb{D}^{\lambda}_{\lambda+P}\mathbb{L}^{\lambda+P}_{\lambda+P}(-\langle P\sqcup Q\rangle_{\lambda+P})

where the final equality follows since
T={\sf rt}(\langle P\sqcup Q\rangle_{\lambda+P})
.

Figure 25.
Examples of adjacent paths
{\color[rgb]{0,1,1}P}\in{\rm DAdd}_{1}(\lambda)
,
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}Q}\in{\rm DRem}_{0}(\lambda)

and
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{\sf rt}({Q})}\in{\rm DAdd}_{1}(\lambda)
.
The three subcases depicted from left-to-right are

(i)

{\color[rgb]{0,1,1}P}\prec{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{\sf rt}({Q})}
are non-commuting

(ii)

{\color[rgb]{0,1,1}P}
and
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{\sf rt}({Q})}
are commuting
and

(iii)

{\color[rgb]{0,1,1}P}={\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{\sf rt}({Q})}
.

Subcase
(ii)
.
We now assume that
P
and
{\sf rt}(Q)
commute. Or equivalently, that
P
is to the left of
Q
as in the central diagram in
Figure

25
.
We have that

(7.10)

\displaystyle{\mathbbm{1}}_{\lambda}\mathbb{D}(+{\sf rt}(Q))\mathbb{D}(-{\sf rt}(Q))\mathbb{D}(+P)

\displaystyle={\mathbbm{1}}_{\lambda}\mathbb{D}(+P)\mathbb{D}(+{\sf rt}(Q))\mathbb{D}(-{\sf rt}(Q))

by two applications of the commuting relation (
6.3
).
Substituting (
7.10
) into (
7.6
) we obtain the following

\displaystyle\mathbb{L}^{\lambda}_{\lambda}(-Q)\mathbb{D}^{\lambda}_{\lambda+P}

\displaystyle=-{\mathbbm{1}}_{\lambda}\mathbb{D}(+P)\mathbb{L}^{\lambda+P}_{\lambda+P}-(-1)^{b({\sf rt}(Q))}{\mathbbm{1}}_{\lambda}\mathbb{D}(+P)\mathbb{D}(+{\sf rt}(Q))\mathbb{D}(-{\sf rt}(Q))

\displaystyle=\mathbb{D}^{\lambda}_{\lambda+P}(-\mathbb{L}^{\lambda+P}_{\lambda+P}-(-1)^{b({\sf rt}(Q))}\mathbb{D}^{\lambda}_{\lambda+P}\mathbb{D}_{\lambda+{\sf rt}(Q)+P}^{\lambda+P}\mathbb{D}^{\lambda+{\sf rt}(Q)+P}_{\lambda+P})

\displaystyle=\mathbb{D}^{\lambda}_{\lambda+P}\mathbb{L}^{\lambda+P}_{\lambda+P}(-\langle P\sqcup Q\rangle_{\lambda+P}).

where the final equality follows as
{\sf rt}(Q)={\sf rt}(\langle P\sqcup Q\rangle_{\lambda+P})
.

Subcase
(iii)
.
We now assume that
P={\sf rt}(Q)
, as in the rightmost diagram in
Figure

25
.
We have that

(7.11)

\displaystyle{\mathbbm{1}}_{\lambda}\mathbb{D}(+{\sf rt}(Q))\mathbb{D}(-{\sf rt}(Q))\mathbb{D}(+P)

\displaystyle=2(-1)^{b(P)+1}{\mathbbm{1}}_{\lambda}\mathbb{D}(+P)\mathbb{L}_{\lambda+P}^{\lambda+P}

by cubic relation (
6.6
).
Substituting (
7.11
) into (
7.6
) we obtain the following

\displaystyle\mathbb{L}^{\lambda}_{\lambda}(-Q)\mathbb{D}^{\lambda}_{\lambda+P}

\displaystyle=-{\mathbbm{1}}_{\lambda}\mathbb{D}(+P)\mathbb{L}^{\lambda+P}_{\lambda+P}-2(-1)^{2b({\sf rt}(Q))+1}{\mathbbm{1}}_{\lambda}\mathbb{D}(+P)\mathbb{L}_{\lambda+P}^{\lambda+P}

\displaystyle=(-1+2){\mathbbm{1}}_{\lambda}\mathbb{D}(+P)\mathbb{L}^{\lambda+P}_{\lambda+P}

\displaystyle=\mathbb{D}^{\lambda}_{\lambda+P}\mathbb{L}^{\lambda+P}_{\lambda+P}(-\langle P\sqcup Q\rangle_{\lambda+P})

where the first two equalities follow as
b({\sf rt}(Q))=b(P)
and so the signs cancel;
the third equality follows as
\langle P\sqcup Q\rangle_{\lambda+P}\in{\rm DRem}_{0}(\lambda)
is the rightmost such removable Dyck path.
∎

Proposition 7.4
.

For
P,Q\in{\rm DRem}(\lambda)
we have that

(7.12)

\mathbb{L}^{\lambda}_{\lambda}(-P)\mathbb{L}^{\lambda}_{\lambda}(-Q)=\mathbb{L}^{\lambda}_{\lambda}(-Q)\mathbb{L}^{\lambda}_{\lambda}(-P)

and moreover, if
P\in{\rm DRem}_{0}(\lambda)
then we have that

(\mathbb{L}^{\lambda}_{\lambda}(-P))^{2}=0.

Proof.

As in previous proofs, we assume that
m=n
(the
m\neq n
case is similar, but easier).
We first suppose that
{\sf last}(Q)=m-1
. In which case, we have that

\mathbb{L}^{\lambda}_{\lambda}(-Q)=\mathbb{L}^{\lambda}_{\lambda}
by
Definition

6.3
.
For any
Q\neq P\in{\rm DRem}(\lambda)
one can check that

\mathbb{L}^{\lambda}_{\lambda}(-P)\mathbb{L}^{\lambda}_{\lambda}=\mathbb{L}^{\lambda}_{\lambda}\mathbb{L}^{\lambda}_{\lambda}(-P)

by expanding out
\mathbb{L}^{\lambda}_{\lambda}(-P)
as prescribed by
Definition

6.3
and then noticing that
\mathbb{L}^{\lambda}_{\lambda}
commutes past every single term in the expansion, by relation (
6.7
).
On the other hand if
P=Q
with
{\sf last}(Q)=m-1
, then
\mathbb{L}^{\lambda}_{\lambda}(-Q)^{2}=0
by relation (
6.7
).
Therefore we can assume for the remainder of the proof that
{\sf last}(P)\leqslant{\sf last}(Q)<m
.

Case 1.
For
P,Q\in{\rm DRem}_{0}(\lambda)
, we claim that

(7.13)

\mathbb{L}^{\lambda}_{\lambda}(-P)\mathbb{L}^{\lambda}_{\lambda}(-Q)=(1-\delta_{PQ})\mathbb{L}^{\lambda}_{\lambda}(-Q)\mathbb{L}^{\lambda}_{\lambda}(-P).

We first consider the case that
P=Q\in{\rm DRem}_{0}(\lambda)
.
We have that

\displaystyle\mathbb{L}^{\lambda}_{\lambda}(-P)^{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(-\mathbb{L}^{\lambda}_{\lambda}-(-1)^{b({\sf rt}(P))}\mathbb{D}(+{\sf rt}(P))\mathbb{D}(-{\sf rt}(P)))^{2}

\displaystyle=
